mr president, in the previous plenary i asked for a minute's silence to be observed in this house on the unjust deaths of children and other civilians in iraq and afghanistan by the bombing of the occupying forces, and this has been done in this sitting. the chairman of that meeting replied that he would put the matter to the bureau. so i want to know if you have discussed this and if you have asked for a minute's silence.

vice-president of the commission. (es) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we have had too many opportunities in recent days to take an interest in the problem of air transport safety. before i turn to the subject of transatlantic flights, i should also like to express my sorrow at the terrible crash of the flash airlines plane on 3 january, which claimed 148 lives, most of them european citizens. i'm sure we all share the feelings of the families affected. but first of all i want to remind you that, once again, we have to wait for the conclusions of the investigation to know exactly the circumstances in which it occurred. over the last few years, aviation safety has been one of the main concerns and concerns of the commission, myself personally and the whole of the directorate-general for transport. it is an area in which we have made clear progress: we have adopted the directive on occurrence reporting in civil aviation; we have approved the creation of the european aviation safety agency, which is already operational; we have approved the rules on certification and maintenance of aircraft to be applied by the agency; and we have also made progress on the 'one sky' initiative, which is an element that should strengthen safety in the aviation sector in europe. unfortunately, all these measures do not apply in this case - i do not know whether they apply unfortunately. this is a different case. there were known problems with the aircraft - for example, the incidents when it was diverted for technical reasons to athens airport - and it was logical to think that they would have been recorded in the database provided for in the directive which you approved last year, but it must be said that the directive will not come into force for another year. as for the air safety agency, which has been operating since 28 september, its responsibility with regard to national certification powers is limited to vessels registered in the community, that is to say, those flying a community flag and therefore does not cover vessels such as those of flash airlines. under the chicago convention, it is the flag state - the state in which the vessels are registered - which is responsible for ensuring that these aircraft meet and comply with all international safety standards, all under the supervision of the international civil aviation organization. however, member states reserve the right to verify that aircraft from third countries flying to them comply with these standards. to facilitate this surveillance, the union states and some european third countries set up in 1996 a system of aircraft inspection and the exchange of information on aircraft inspection, called safa, within the european civil aviation conference (ecac). the european commission co-finances this programme and participates as an observer. this programme has enabled the exchange of information on the six inspections carried out by france, germany and poland following the swiss ban on the boeing 737s of flash airlines. however, although some 3 000 inspections are carried out each year, there is no guarantee that each member state carries out these inspections and how they are carried out. moreover, this system does not guarantee the adoption of collective measures or, above all, the transparency of inspections and measures and, moreover, it lacks mechanisms for sanctioning states which do not comply with the rules. moreover, i believe that this lack of transparency is detrimental to the most security-compliant companies, since this compliance is not known or the highest quality of the most serious companies is not known and gives opportunities to those that are less serious. on the contrary, greater transparency would mean that everyone would know what is going on and, of course, the passenger, the consumer, could and would clearly choose maximum safety. the commission is aware of these shortcomings in the current organisation of the aircraft control system. i would also like to remind you that following an accident similar to that of flash airlines, which occurred in 1996 in puerto plata, dominican republic, on a flight operated by a turkish charter company on behalf of a german travel agency, the commission presented in 1997 a proposal for a directive on the control of aircraft from third countries landing at community airports. i would also like to point out that the commission submitted an amended proposal on this text in january 2002, which essentially took up the 1997 text with corrections that improved the text itself and also allowed it to be adopted quickly. we are at the moment, as the honourable members are aware, in the conciliation procedure phase, which i hope will be moving forward in the coming days, and the flash airlines accident shows how absolutely necessary and urgent this text is: firstly, it is clear that the time limit for transposing the directive must be as short as possible - recent events show this - and that we must not exceed the usual two years for transposing directives into national law. no one would understand that after what has just happened there would be further delay on the part of the states of the union on this issue. as for the annual report which the commission is to publish, i think it should be made available to all interested parties. it should provide the data necessary for the choice of airlines by travel organisers to encourage them to pay even more attention to safety issues. of course, the data in this report can only reflect the reality at a given time and do not always allow general conclusions to be drawn about the safety or non-safety of a particular appliance or appliances in general from a particular company. however, the british initiative to publish a list of airlines that are not allowed to access british airspace shows that transparency is not only possible, but is going to happen anyway. and the commission's report must be drawn up and designed with this in mind. the commission supports parliament when it proposes that a measure adopted by a member state - after an inspection - should have effects throughout the community. the attitude of the most stringent state in the union will thus be the attitude of the union as a whole towards this type of aircraft. this accident does not only require an effort and a reinforcement of controls and the transparency of these controls. it also requires us to consider the need for greater discipline on the part of tour operators in informing passengers about the airline they are to fly with and about the choice of airline. it is not clear why, when you take a package tour, you are given all kinds of information about the hotel, the meal plan, sometimes even about the places you are going to visit, offers of complementary activities, but you are not usually told exactly which airline will be responsible for the flight. i have asked the commission services to give priority consideration to this question, which i consider essential. this dramatic matter, mr president, is not the only one which has caused controversy and concern about safety issues - in the sense of security - among the public in the civil aviation sector, since we have all been aware during this christmas of matters relating to the suspension of flights from european countries to the united states, with the corresponding problems and difficulties for all those travellers who had planned to take these planes. the current situation undoubtedly warrants closer and more active cooperation with the american authorities to strengthen security aspects in the face of new terrorist risks. but, while we share these objectives, we are often confronted with measures which are adopted unilaterally and, in this connection, i would like to say that, on several occasions, the commission has made clear to the american authorities the need for dialogue and agreement prior to decision-making, which would not only facilitate the implementation of decisions but also offer better results, taking into account the experience which, fortunately or unfortunately, some member states have in this field. the difficult negotiations conducted by mr bolkenstein on the automatic transfer of personal data to the american authorities also show that in the end mutually acceptable solutions can be found, although this takes longer and creates more difficulties. in this connection, and speaking of the transfer of data, i should like to say that last december i had the opportunity to present to the libe committee, together with mr vitorino, mr patten and mr bolkenstein, the results of these negotiations. as we said then, we believe that the results obtained on the transfer and storage of personal data enable the commission to prepare an adequacy finding in line with the data protection directive, which will complement an initiative within the icao to create an international framework and adequate information for passengers. it must be clear that the recent behaviour of our american friend and partner does not concern these points. we have to say that the data which has caused some difficulties was not the data which appears in the passenger name record (pnr) and which is detailed enough to avoid certain types of errors which have occurred in this field. however, even if the cancellation of flights by air france and british airways, for example, is not proved to be justified a posteriori, one must always be cautious in the face of the threat of terrorism and it is even better to be overly cautious in the face of the fact. i would also like to mention another matter which has been reported in the media and which is causing some concern, namely the presence of security personnel on board aircraft travelling to the united states, which raises all kinds of problems. first of all, i would point out that this is a matter for the member states. that there is no community competence in this field. secondly, i would point out that although the commission has arranged a meeting of the aviation group on 16 january, i.e. at the end of this week, in which all the civil aviation directors of the member states of the union are taking part, this item will be added to the long list of items on the agenda and will be discussed. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of passengers is not compromised. that safety must be considered in a global way and that it is not enough to talk about guarantees and the safety of the airlines in our territories, that is, of european airlines and aircraft, because aircraft from third countries also arrive at our airports and our citizens travel in such transports en masse. the safety of these aircraft must therefore be strengthened and there is scope for this and i hope that there will be agreement between parliament and the council on this point of the safety of third country aircraft. finally, i would like to say that in order to avoid problems of terrorism in the civil aviation sector, safety matters must be dealt with from the point of view of maximum guarantees and safety for passengers and citizens. in any case, we must not forget that the number of air accidents has fallen by 45% in the last 10 years, while air traffic has doubled. however, with 576 fatalities on commercial flights worldwide in 2003, we must continue to work to prevent these types of accidents from happening and to achieve an even greater reduction in them. mr president, the flash airlines incident was a real tragedy and i happened to experience the tragedy of this incident personally, as i was in france and saw the reactions of french society. i would like to point out that the european parliament can take great satisfaction in the fact that for a long time it has been aware of the problem of air safety. the commission's attitude in the case of the european aviation safety agency is typical of this direction, where we have supported the project from the outset and this year we have voted in the budget all the funds needed to staff this agency quickly. at the same time, parliament has been very firm from the start on the maes report on third country aircraft, which the commission rightly took the initiative to promote, and i am glad that mrs de palacio is now agreeing with the european parliament's position on the differences we have with the council on the conciliation procedure. it is very important, and i hope the council will take note, that the arrangements made on this matter are the right ones and that we ensure a high level of safety not only for our own aircraft but also for those of third countries coming into the european union. beyond that, you know there's the issue that was created with security after the 9/11 terrorist attack. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i believe that on the other side of the atlantic, although i understand the reaction, there is at the same time a haughty attitude towards europe, a willingness to deal with the problems unilaterally. i think we will make it clear to the other side that we want to cooperate, but at the same time we want to be equal partners with them, and in this direction the european commission can help decisively.
the proposal for a regulation on the chartering of cargo and passenger ships is part of the general anti-union policy of the european union. under the pretext of enhancing competitiveness, it aims to increase the profits of shipowners by reducing the wages and social rights of shipowners and by providing scandalous tax breaks for shipping companies in the member states of the european union. the recommendation in the explanatory memorandum to the regulation that member states reconsider the creation of a european ship register is a strategic choice by the capital to increase its profitability. the commission had already put forward this proposal in 1989. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the safety of ships is not compromised by the fact that the shipowners are not able to carry out their work in the same way as the shipowners. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud in the maritime sector. the proposal to strengthen the criminal framework for the suppression of pollution from ships is not going to reduce maritime crime, since everything will be subject to safeguarding and increasing the profits of ship owners, of big capital in general, which controls the sources of energy and the means of transport and production. we call on the european parliament to intervene to force the p&i club and the shipowners of the prestige and tasman spirit to pay the fines for the two shipwrecks, so that their crews, held as hostages in spain and pakistan respectively, can be released. mr president, the kke rejects all three motions for a regulation ... (the speaker is interrupted by the president)
mr president, it is truly astonishing to see once again that the bulk of the problem of marine pollution does not come from shipwrecks themselves but from illegal dumping of ships, whether it be oil or waste in general, and that this problem has not been dealt with as it should be. there is the marpol convention, its application in the member states is different in terms of the sanctions imposed, and for this reason the initiative of the european commission is useful so that there is a uniform approach to the issue in terms of sanctions. the commission has already adopted a directive on this subject, namely directive 2059 on ship-generated waste reception facilities, but it has not been fully implemented by the member states. this directive must be implemented. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the environment is protected, and i would like to ask the commissioner to take this opportunity to explain why the commission has not taken the necessary steps to ensure that the environment is protected. so here we are taking a positive step, and what i regret to point out is that the council, while it had asked the european commission to bring in this regulation on penalties in cases of illegal dumping of waste from ships, now does not seem to be willing to move quickly, does not seem to be willing to accept the proposals of the commission and parliament and seems to be delaying the whole process. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done.
commission. - mr president, i should first like to thank your assembly, and in particular mr philippe herzog, your rapporteur, for the excellent work on this complex and delicate issue which the commission has raised in its green paper on services of general interest. the commission considers that public services are at the heart of the debate on the future of european integration. all the countries of the union have public services which are part of their own traditions, in their own particular situations, and in particular at local level. we consider services of general interest to be an essential component of the european social model. we believe that these services contribute to the quality of life of our citizens, to the competitiveness of our industries and to the strengthening of our social and territorial cohesion. the same is true for the future member states: the proper functioning of services of general interest is essential to facilitate their integration. with the green paper of may last, the commission embarked for the first time on a comprehensive review of community policies on services of general interest. this reflection was necessary, in particular, following your own request, made in the langen report of november 2001, which had opened a debate on the question of whether or not it was appropriate to create a general legal framework for services of general interest at community level. we are currently finalising the analysis of the contributions received during the public consultation. we have received today almost 300 contributions, many of them very substantial, and which obviously deserve further work. it would therefore be premature at this stage to draw any precise conclusions from this work. nevertheless, we intend to draw political conclusions before the end of this parliamentary term, following a request which was unanimously expressed in your committee on economic and monetary affairs. of course, it will be necessary to decide what kind of instrument the commission can propose to ensure quality public services in europe. the possibilities include a framework directive with a number of common principles: equal access for users, universality, continuity, adaptability and evaluation. i know that this is the subject of lively debate in your house. we can also think of the adoption of a white paper, pending the constitutional treaty, which, on this point, formulates what we consider to be some progress. in the meantime, a white paper could define the political framework for our future action or set out the way forward for the sectoral approach. in addition, we are continuing work on other cases concerning services of general interest, in particular the application of the state aid rules, following the altmark judgment. the public consultation on this point is clear. we need more certainty in this area, and in particular a notification exemption and sufficient exemption coverage, given the number of cases potentially affected by the application of the altmark case-law. in conclusion, our plan is to lay the foundations for the community to play a more proactive role in providing high quality services of general interest to citizens and businesses. this approach must of course take account of the principle of subsidiarity, and our aim is to enable the design of a coherent policy for services of general interest within the union, which will provide the best articulation of the relations and responsibilities between the different levels of government. your resolution will be an essential and indispensable contribution to the definition of this approach, and the commission is therefore looking forward to its outcome with great impatience.
the committee on legal affairs and the internal market, draftsman of the opinion of the committee on legal affairs and the internal market. mr president, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr herzog, for the good cooperation we have had in the preparation of the report to be voted on, which was done with the strong cooperation of the economic and legal affairs committees. i would like to express my regret, however, at the outcome of the vote in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, which reversed the basic proposals of the committee on legal affairs. the approach adopted jointly by the rapporteur and myself is not blind to the problems posed by modern reality. the key issue is not the ownership of utilities. it is clear that modern developments allow for private-public cooperation. however, in order to avoid a negative effect of privatisation, the basic rules governing the mission of public utilities, including the basic principles of their operation and financing, must be respected. it is important, among other things, that the needs of the population as a whole are taken into account in all cases. the principles of universality, continuity, affordability and quality must therefore be safeguarded at community level. our difference with the liberal approach is that privatisation is not an end in itself and that it should in no case mean a complete dependence of public services on market rules, a dependence which goes so far as to exclude even the financing by local government of public services serving local communities. in particular, for certain services, such as health, education and social security, national authorities should have the right to determine the rules governing the provision of these services by private profit-making bodies. i therefore call on this house, and especially on those who believe in the european social model as part of the european production system, to vote in favour of the amendments relating to the above. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens and mutagens is a very important one. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a very good one.
mr president, the proposed arrangements are designed to make it easier to hand over public services of general interest to private interests. in a time of economic crisis, the only certainty is that big business wants to get their hands on public wealth to maximize their profits. in addition to their important development role for the national economy, utilities also play a strategic role in relation to the security and independence of each country, especially in times of economic and other crises. the privatisation of these companies and the liberalisation of their markets will lead to a limitation of this growth and, certainly, to the enrichment of their shareholders instead of strengthening the national economy. it is not true that market liberalization is a factor in technological progress and economic efficiency, because, according to its practical application to date, it leads rather to the opposite result. privatizations have led to the destruction of productive forces, poor performance, degradation of the quality of services provided, appalling social inequalities, rising prices and reduced employment. privatizations have had adverse effects on workers, not only by increasing the prices of services provided but, above all, by waves of redundancies and the subsequent replacement of labour regimes by flexible forms of work. the restrictions imposed on big business to operate in the public interest and the good intentions to ensure certain principles governing the operation and services offered, even if they are applied initially, will then degenerate, with the citizens as the only victims. we are opposed to the selling off of public wealth to the benefit of big business. we support the public nature of public utilities, which must be upgraded, offering good and cheap services to the working people, and become a lever of a popular economy.
in the light of the recent developments in the field of nuclear safety, the commission has decided to extend the scope of the directive to include the disposal of radioactive waste. in fact, apart from a few general points of view which apply to all types of waste, the proposal for a directive makes no substantial reference to the special and sensitive nature of radioactive waste in terms of public health. the proposals to build deep geological deposits within specific time limits are so ill-founded that they can only be regarded as empty talk. the report by the committee on industrial policy, external economic relations, research and energy makes an effort to support the flawed text of the directive with comments which are either neutral or downright negative. the report accepts, for example, the transfer of spent nuclear waste to third countries, which we are absolutely opposed to. the proposal for a directive and the report by the committee on industry confirm once again our assessment that there is a huge gap between the seriousness and depth of the environmental problems created by the uncontrolled activities of big business and the measures to solve them. that is why the members of parliament from the communist party of greece are voting against the relevant texts.

the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. i'll make this quick. i would like to thank you for the constructive contributions which we have heard a great many of today. i would just like to comment on the specific agricultural questions and then my colleague pascal lamy will round things off accordingly. the question was raised whether the usa was showing more flexibility on the cotton question. well, you know that bob zoellick has published a letter to all wto members. in this letter he deals specifically with the cotton question, he devotes a whole chapter to this question, and from the way he presents it it is quite clear that there is a certain openness in this question. in any event, it is open not only to changes in export refunds - that they should be abandoned - and to market opening and less distortive internal support - but also to the idea that means should be developed to better assist the particularly poor countries, which are unilaterally dependent on cotton production, in the field of development aid. i can only repeat what mrs gonzález said about cotton production and the importance of cotton for europe: it is true that european production accounts for only about 2% of world market production. it is also true that we do not apply any export subsidies and that we have fully opened our cotton markets to the least developed countries in the world in the context of the eba decision. and as you know, there is a proposal to change our cotton market regulation which will lead to less distortive measures in the cotton sector. so a mix of blue-box and green-box measures. i am trying to get a decision in the council of agriculture ministers in march. as far as japan's rice policy is concerned, everyone knows that rice is a very sensitive issue for japan and has always been considered as such. however, we note that japan has recently entered into bilateral negotiations with a number of wto member states. the question of rice is certainly on the agenda of these bilateral negotiations and is part of these negotiations, which is why the japanese have begun in geneva to adopt a somewhat more - let us say - constructive or less strict stance on this issue in general. if it has been said that we have no mandate to propose that export refunds be abolished entirely for certain products, that is simply and gravely wrong, because it is explicitly stated in our mandate that we can very well propose this for different products. mrs mann asked me about our sugar proposal. we are working hard on preparing a proposal and i have already announced that my aim is to put the proposal in the commission in the middle of this year and then to send it to parliament and the council. in conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, just one more remark. i would like to invite parliament to participate in the intensification of the dialogue which we proposed in our opening statements. we need more, we need more intensive discussions, and i would ask you in particular to intensify the discussions with the ngos, because it is precisely in the case of some ngos that ideas are sometimes totally contrary to european interests. i would like to invite you to consider that waiting is bad for us, for the european union, and for european agriculture. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
the commission is aware of the fact that the european parliament has been involved in the negotiations on the maastricht treaty and that the council has been involved in the negotiations on the maastricht treaty. our main objectives in this negotiation remain the same: first, a further step towards opening up trade; second, better rules for the multilateral framework for trade; and, finally, better integration of developing countries, which have so far benefited only very unevenly from the increase in international trade. if we try to translate all this, to answer your questions, into the following words: what must be done, what should be our objective for 2004, the answer is quite simple. our objective for 2004 is to achieve by the summer what could not be achieved in cancun, that is to say, to go two thirds of the way through this negotiation. this is our political objective for 2004. so you will tell me, in view of what i said in the introduction to the debate on the state of the art in geneva, that such an objective requires energy and acceleration, without a doubt. compared to the present level of work and pressure in the geneva boiler, this requires an acceleration and we will, like others, put coal in the geneva boiler to make things go further. from this point of view, i said it and i say it again in reply to mrs mann's question, the letter that my american counterpart circulated in recent days, and which he commented on in the press yesterday, is good news in that it is the culmination of a thesis that was beginning to spread and that 2004 - an election year in the united states - would be a lost year. well, no, you won't. 2004 may well not be a lost year and it is important that two of the big elephants of world trade, the united states and europe, agree to this. so, of course, to achieve this we will have to get active and we will, as we have started to do since our mandate was renewed, work with the g20, with the g90, with the united states, with japan, to name only the main players of the moment, within a variable geometry that allows us to advance our own position under the best conditions. as franz fischer rightly said, in this enterprise of work and entertainment, to raise the temperature, we need you and the contacts you have with your colleagues in each of the large blocks i have just mentioned. that is what i had to say in response to the overwhelmingly supportive position expressed during this debate. a few words to answer some critics. mr lannoye blames us for two things: firstly, we have not reconstructed our mandate, we have only repositioned it, and secondly, there are traces of the so-called singapore issues within this mandate. indeed, mr lannoye is right, we have not rebuilt our mandate, we have repositioned it. and if there is a lesson to be learned from what happened between cancun and today, it is that no one around the wto table asked for a renegotiation of the negotiating agenda that had been agreed in doha. that is the programme on the table and that is the programme to be negotiated. as regards singapore, it is true that we have softened our position, we have given priority to trade facilitation and transparency in public procurement for reasons which the green group should understand as it is committed to transparency. in a number of cases, this would be very beneficial to trade facilitation, particularly for small and mediumsized enterprises which unfortunately do not often have the means to pay the hedge-breakers that large multinational groups can afford for procedures, customs and other networks that allow decisions to be made faster from time to time. i would simply say to mrs figueiredo and mr désir once again that their concerns about public services are unfounded. my mandate on this point is clear and unambiguous. i have never deviated from it and i have no intention of doing so. i now come to two specific points in reply to the remarks of mrs ferrer and mrs corbey. to mrs ferrer, on the textile sector, we are prepared to reduce what remains of industrial protection on our textile tariffs on the condition, of course, that others do the same, which is part of the negotiation. i would say to mrs corbey, who was wondering where we were with access to medicines, that we are working on it in the two areas where the rest of the operations are now taking place. firstly in geneva, where we have to transpose the terms of the august agreement into the trips agreement and we are working on that. this is not a matter of course, because the balance achieved in august between the various parts and the various requirements must be reflected in a text which will now be a legal text, and this obviously requires a little work. we are also working on this within the european union, as we want, as a sort of anticipation, to adjust our own compulsory licensing system in our european legislation to adapt it to this new agreement at the wto and to allow easier access possibly in a number of developing countries. we will do so in the form of a proposal for a recommendation to the council of ministers, since, pending the entry into force of european patent legislation, which is decided but will not take effect for a number of years, we must, in order to put this agreement on access to medicines into effect, amend existing national legislation one by one. so we are going to have the council adopt, if it agrees, and we have every reason to believe that it will, a recommendation to ensure that the necessary adaptations are made in each of the national laws, pending, of course, the updating of the european system, which, i remind you, will only come into force in a few years' time. these are the answers i wanted to give at this point, thanking you for your attention.

commission. mr president, we lacked the imagination, when preparing my statement, to include the aspect of hunting in europe. i see that quite simply as being outside the scope of what one would normally expect this initiative to cover and fully agree with mr de rossa, mr corrie and others who have addressed this misunderstanding. we have enough real problems to cope with. the remarks made by mrs mathieu and others about hunting not being the only threat to these species are very important to bear in mind. there are two separate aspects: that of a deliberate threat, driven by greed, and that of a threat that is driven by people's need to survive. both are equally dangerous for the species we are talking about. in this discussion i see a parallel with the difficult issue of the need for africans to change and move from a pastoral lifestyle, where they roam the countryside grazing their cattle, but in which there is an imbalance between the number of people and cattle and the square kilometres available. it is this pressure on land use, reflecting urbanisation and population growth, which many people do not expect to be a problem in a place as big as africa. however, when one looks at the environmental constraints, such as access to water, africa is not so big. the pressure on forests is one such systemic, ongoing and dramatic pressure which presents perhaps the greatest obstacle to establishing a sustainable alternative because the issue has to do with poverty and with real problems. we should mount a strong front to tackle the problem of criminal acts driven by greed. trying to convince logging companies to act within the law and to adhere to regulations, and for this to be more widely recognised by governments, is part of the drive to create a minimum of decency in matters relating to forestry in african countries. this is quite clearly part of the action and our policy. finally, there are many aspects to the trade issue. one is quite simply our own legitimate defence of our health standards. we stopped imports of nile perch from lake victoria for a number of years until we successfully managed – and this is one of my pet cases – to create an effective phytosanitary control. these exports are now up and running again and we have recreated thousands of jobs on a sustainable basis. i find it a little strange, however, that we have not done anything similar when it comes to bushmeat. what people have said in this debate is quite correct: ebola is one threat and there are many other very difficult aspects of this trade which require considerable attention. at the same time, many people depend for their survival on being able to hunt and eat bushmeat in their normal, traditional environment. we should respect that. we have had the debate about the brain drain. maybe we can start discussing the protein drain, where the temptation to get our hands on exotic protein should be countered by focusing greater public attention on the issues involved. i would like to thank parliament and the rapporteur for this initiative.

commission. the agreement which we have been discussing with our syrian colleagues but which has not yet been initialled would, in my judgment, provide a solid platform for economic and political reform in syria. the dismantlement of tariffs and the opening of markets will be an important incentive for syria to undertake legal and institutional reforms. the agreement would also help syria to integrate into the world economy and could be a stepping stone towards other initiatives, such as syrian membership of the world trade organization. the provisions on the respect for human rights, on non-proliferation and on cooperation against terrorism would, i trust, strengthen our ability to engage with syria on these issues. as regards the facilitation of regional integration, the agreement would complete the network of euro-mediterranean association agreements with our mediterranean partners. it constitutes the final step for the euro-mediterranean free trade area envisaged by the barcelona declaration. we should now continue working together to ensure trade liberalisation between the mediterranean partner countries themselves. the agreement would be the most important element in our policy of constructive engagement with syria. it is our belief that the agreement should strengthen the incentives for reform in syria, both by creating a more open economy and society and by establishing a political dialogue on issues such as respect for democratic principles and human rights. i believe that the united states shares our fundamental objective of integrating syria into the international community as a country that abides by international law and cooperates, we hope, on non-proliferation and against terrorism. the association agreement should be seen as a framework for a long-term engagement with syria in this respect.
the commission is aware of the fact that the council has not yet adopted a decision on the conclusion of the cooperation agreement with syria. but i wanted to ask you to clarify the last part of my question: do you share the views expressed by the united states on relations with syria and its role in the region and the achievement of peace? the method followed by the european union is different from that followed by the united states. the objective may be common in the larger context, but the methodology is different. do you share the fears specifically expressed by the united states? do these fears have anything to do with european union policy?
commission. the commission welcomes parliament’s interest in its report on the programme for action. we share the overall assessment that substantial progress has been made, but further action is needed – by developing countries, by eu member states and other donors, by the business sector, by ngos and by european institutions – to confront and fight hiv/aids, tb and malaria. we recognise that these diseases affect different people in different ways and that women, children and the poorest sections of the population are often most vulnerable and most severely hit. this should be documented, wherever practically possible, by developing countries, with support from the international donor community. at the same time, we should avoid portraying women only as victims and make an effort to recognise the tremendous contribution they make in terms of caring for people living with hiv/aids or for children left behind. women comprise that group of the population which is most affected, in a negative sense, by poverty and disease but strangely, at the same time, that group shows the biggest potential for change and hope. i note with satisfaction the importance attached in the report to our cooperation with the world health organization. this is now recognised more visibly through the strategic partnership which is being developed between our two institutions and which, for example, has led to the signature of an agreement to provide eur 25 million of community funding to support the who in developing its pharmaceutical policies. i also understand the emphasis placed by the rapporteur on access to basic health services and on the wish to see increased funding for this sector. the commission is open to increasing – however we can – support for health and other social services. however, this is a demand-driven process in which our partner countries are in the lead. as to the global fund, our contribution has been steadily increasing, with the vital support of parliament. we have now secured funding for the next four years, both from the community budget and from the european development fund. despite this, resources for the global fund are still far from adequate. we are committed to working together with the member states to find a sustainable solution. the eu as a whole – the member states and the commission – have so far contributed more than 50% of the money in the global fund. that is a figure worth keeping in mind in view of the complaints made now and then by other donor countries. access to affordable medicines is an important element in our programme for action. through tiered pricing and the untying of aid we have managed to contribute to a process whereby prices on medicines have been reduced by up to 90%. supply of medicines at tiered prices is essential and the european union has adopted a regulation to prevent the re-importation into europe of medicines sold at tiered prices in developing countries. that is very important. the report rightly points to the important role that research and development of new medicines and vaccines could play in fighting these three 'killer diseases'. more needs to be done in public and private research to develop new products which target these diseases. it is clear that pharmaceutical companies could play a constructive role in addressing these needs. the european and developing countries clinical trials partnership aims to facilitate such a contribution from industry. the commission is already contributing to this initiative, but we also expect member states and industry to allocate resources. i should like to respond to mrs sandbæk's remarks concerning the negative impact of the mexico city policy carried out by the bush administration in washington. i agree with the choice of wording put forward by mrs sandbæk. we have reacted to this policy, which was announced when president bush took office, by saying clearly that europe is ready and willing to fill the decency gap, and that is what we are doing.

(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) the aim of the report is to strengthen a few large fisheries undertakings by means of economic and institutional measures so that they can improve their competitiveness. the rapporteur justifies the increase in these quotas by referring to the undeniably high nutritional value of the product, its economic value, employment, conservation of resources, etc. these reasons cover the fact that this measure concerns a few large companies in the eu and that the developed countries also overexploit the world's marine resources, which is harmful to the poor countries and developing countries, as the international fisheries agreements are unfair and guarantee the rights of the strong. if this report, which deals with subsidies for the modernisation of the fishing fleet, export subsidies, customs duties and technocratic barriers to imports, is adopted, it will provokingly strengthen the ownership of specific enterprises and make the international division of labour even more unfair, with the poor countries and the developing countries losing out. i would point out that the report rightly calls the withdrawal of large tuna vessels unproductive and disastrous, and therefore proposes that they be abolished and replaced by fleet modernisation. but the same subsidies for the withdrawal of small and medium-sized coastal fishing vessels and medium-sized fishing vessels are, according to the eu, structural funds supposedly designed to balance the respective sectors. this contradiction shows once again the class policy pursued by the ruling circles of the european union. it affects small and mediumsized fishermen and benefits the large capitalist fisheries.
in writing. - (el) the there are several points in the report in question with which we agree. for reasons of principle, we agree that it is necessary to guarantee the right of the peoples of europe and the world to cultural self-determination and cultural diversity. the report is partly correct in formulating some of the causes of the growing cultural homogenization and commercialization (pressure from the wto, internal competition both between the us and the eu and within the eu, the inability of unesco to fulfil its role, etc.). we believe that cultural diversity is one of the last safeguards of the historical existence and continuing identity of peoples, and that it is also a historical necessity (which the report also recognizes), and we therefore consider the measures proposed to be completely inadequate. the problem cannot be solved within the framework of organisations such as unesco, the council of europe or - especially - the eu. the only way to halt and reverse this course, which is so disastrous for culture, is for the people to take matters into their own hands, to rebel against the idea and practice of market laws dictated by imperialist globalisation and to demand respect for their cultural values. for the reasons stated above, the kke did not support the report and did not take part in the final vote.

council. the council confirms to the honourable member that on 9 december 2003 the european commission and syria reached an understanding at technical level on all remaining issues in the negotiations on the euromed association agreement. when it enters into force, this agreement will replace the existing 1977 cooperation agreement which, as part of the global mediterranean approach, currently links the two sides. the items of the future agreement will now have to be politically approved by both sides, paving the way for ratification by both sides. the council underlines that on the political side the draft agreement intends to provide a framework to conduct regular political dialogue on international issues and mutual interest, fostering mutual understanding and promoting the convergence of views. in particular it includes essential provisions on respect for the democratic principles on fundamental rights enshrined in the universal declaration of human rights; cooperation to counter the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction and their means of delivery in line with the conclusions of the external relations council in november 2003; and the exchange of information and joint efforts to prevent and fight against terrorism, including the implementation of the relevant united nations resolutions. with regard to the second element in the honourable member's question on the middle east peace process in general, the council invites the honourable member to refer to the presidency conclusions of the european council meeting held in brussels on 12 december 2003. in paragraph 61 the european council recalled that a comprehensive peace in the middle east must also include syria and, of course, lebanon. finally, there was a third element in the question. the council has not commented on positions taken on this matter by the united states, nor does the council intend to do so at this stage.

i have to say that i am not in favour of this. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the initiative to draw up this report stems from our shared conviction that the barcelona process needs to be accelerated, and in particular the need to strengthen the european economy and the european labour market by creating new and quality jobs. the service sector covers a wide range of private and public sectors. it is the fastest-developing and most dynamic sector of the european economy. over the last 20 years, about two thirds of the growth in gross domestic product in the european union has come from the services sector, and the largest increase in the employment rate is again in this sector. today services account for about 69% of total employment. the aim of our policy must be to increase this percentage, and it is worth mentioning that in the united states the corresponding figure is 80%. at the same time, we must be aware of the uneven development of the services sector between the countries of the european union. the countries with the most severe structural problems, such as greece and portugal, have the lowest rates of employment in services. the new member states, with the exception of cyprus, have similarly low rates. our policy, both at european and national level, must play an important role in the development of services. effective regulation of services markets is essential to protect consumer interests, preserve quality and guarantee fair competition. however, there are still very rigid regulations, which suffocate economic activity. it is urgent to identify and implement measures with clear objectives in order to ensure a satisfactory level of protection for service providers and for the services themselves, so that they can be established throughout the territory of the european union. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council should be consulted on the future of the european union's external relations. the implementation of these infrastructures depends on the liberalisation of energy and telecommunications markets for easy and cheap access to them and on the transeuropean networks programme to increase market capacity. the committee on employment and social affairs calls on the european commission to present a white paper proposing policies for each sector of employment, so that we can have the implementation of an effective european market soon. the committee calls on the european commission to propose a directive on services in the internal market to tackle the obstacles to the movement of workers and the provision of services. the commission has also proposed that the member states should reduce the bureaucratic procedures for setting up and operating the services. it is necessary to create an appropriate framework of conditions and regulatory, economic and fair competition provisions, to promote new forms of work in the service sector, such as part-time work, teleworking, temporary work, to create conditions for a suitable and continuously trained staff, flexible and productive, to create quality and healthy working conditions and mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is clear that the directive is aimed at further liberalising the labour market to the benefit of big business and, at the same time, it is clearly damaging the labour and professional rights of the few categories of workers who still retain such rights. in this context, the aim is to maximise the ability of firms to secure a surplus of skilled labour, to select as much as they need and when they need it, to depress the price of labour, to infringe workers' rights and gains and, more generally, to enhance the exploitation of scientific manpower. at the same time, it is an indirect but clear interference in the education system of the member states, despite the contrary provision of the treaty on european union. the separation of professional and academic equivalence is the means of enforcing an educational model that limits the extent and depth of higher education to the minimum requirements of enterprises, i.e. it is far below the academically accepted level. it is an educational model that degrades higher education as a whole and creates a specialized scientific workforce without depth of knowledge and therefore without requirements. as regards the content of the directive, we note that, under the pretext of simplifying recognition procedures, the minimum conditions for access to the legally protected scientific professions are much lower than those already in force in many countries of the european union and in greece. each member state shall allow the pursuit of a profession to any foreign national or national who has completed training recognized by another member state for the pursuit of the same profession, irrespective of the level of such qualifications. in other words, an engineer graduating from a three-year course automatically acquires the same professional rights as a graduate of a five-year course in greek craft industries. in addition, the directive equates vocationally any post-secondary education qualification, whether acquired at a higher education institution or at a vocational school. even professional experience or traineeship can, according to the directive, be equated with three or four and five years of education. in greece in particular, the directive is intervening indirectly and creating a situation of certainty in order to close the doors on issues relating to education, which are the subject of intense debate and controversy on the part of the parties concerned. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to finance the necessary measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to finance the necessary measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to finance the necessary measures. in order to implement the above and to overcome any obstacles at national level, the directive attempts to regulate in detail every possible case and to exclude any possible derogation. it also sets up a special single committee to recognise professional qualifications and to monitor the implementation of the project. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented. we propose that the academic equivalence of qualifications be made a basic condition for the recognition of professional equivalence, which will be based on the conditions of the host country. we also demand that all laboratories or study centres that are affiliated with foreign universities and sell their degrees be closed down and that the system of university education rights be abolished altogether.
the purpose of the zappalà report, mr president, is very important, because through mobility, which of course requires mutual recognition of qualifications, we are giving workers more opportunities within the european single market. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the quality of higher education is not compromised. in greece there is a particular problem in this respect. because of the lack of private universities, we have a world record of student immigration. in view of this situation, european universities are cooperating with the so-called liberal studies centres in greece. these degrees are not recognized by the state and the social problem of thousands of people is growing. we have raised this matter in the past in the commission, both myself and colleagues on the left, not mr alysandrakis, and the european commission has been clear on this. he says that degrees should be recognized. the same was said recently by the court of justice of the european communities in the neri case in relation to a similar case in italy. however, because the problems of the greek education system are perpetuating themselves, i tabled an amendment which was approved by the committee so that, without any legal obstacles, european diplomas could be recognised one hour earlier and without greece being condemned by the court of justice of the european communities. not only that, but with a legal security regime, renowned european universities should open branches in greece, so that the level of studies can be raised through competition and stop student emigration. in order to avoid misunderstandings as to the level of education, we tabled a second amendment, together with mr trakatelli and mr zappalà, which takes the obvious course, namely that member states are entitled to subject the various institutions which cooperate with universities in other member states to the control mechanisms applicable to their own universities. in greece, in recent days, and after the uproar that has been caused, there has been a change in the positions of the leadership of the socialist party with regard to non-state higher education. the logical consequence of this change is the adoption of our own amendments. i hope that will be reflected in the vote, mr president.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the first is that the movement of selfemployed people, and in particular of those in regulated professions, is being made easier than it has been so far, with a view to developing the internal market and the services sector, which is lagging behind. the second guideline should be to ensure the quality of the services offered during crossborder movement. in this spirit, the main innovations of the proposed directive should be examined, such as the distinction between cross-border provision of services and cross-border establishment, the proposed levels, etc. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on the free movement of goods and services are respected. on the other hand, the innovation of the directive, which introduces the concept of common bases, should be particularly welcomed, because it allows for the active participation of the liberalized operators in the context of strengthening their crossborder cooperation. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's citizens are not forced to move to another member state, and that the european union is not obliged to do so. for this reason, it is important that the provision of degrees certifying the theoretical training of graduates who have completed their studies in several countries, require as a necessary condition that their studies are provided in all countries by recognized educational institutions and not by enterprises that are not covered by the official education system of the host country. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the commission's proposal for a directive on the coordination of the laws of the member states relating to the taking up and pursuit of the business of credit institutions is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr pimenta, for his report. the amendment proposed by mr hajidakis is therefore dangerous.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) the service sector is particularly important for large-scale capital in the eu because industrial activities are increasingly moving to other parts of the world. the kratsa report follows the line required by the conditions imposed by big business on this sector, and the content of the report shows how the eu's neoliberal policy is tackling the problems. the focus is on improving competitiveness, harmonising the service sector with competition policy and the crossborder provision of services, while entrepreneurship is seen as the solution to all problems of unemployment and underemployment. the so-called open method of coordination of national policies is also promoted, and it is nothing less than a mechanism for imposing eu policy without seemingly involving the eu institutions. the report also praises the new forms of work, parttime work and temporary work, while hypocritically talking about improving the quality of these forms. it is argued that the retirement age should be raised and that the 'beneficial' effects of privatising universal services should be recognised. it goes without saying that the standard phrase of improving the conditions of women and the handicapped and using new technology is not lacking either. for these reasons, members of the communist party of greece in the european parliament voted against the report.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) the texts under debate concern the few immigrants who manage to escape the controls carried out by border guards and electronic records with new biometric data, not immigrants arriving on european charter flights so that they can stand in line to become 'legal' immigrants. even for these immigrants, the conditions for obtaining a residence permit, at least in greece, are insurmountable. in other words, people who have been in greece for 10, 20 or 30 years will still be illegal immigrants. the ruling class wants them to be illegal so that it can hold them physically and mentally hostage. the text cynically admits that immigrants are useful in solving the eu's demographic problems, in meeting the need for largely unskilled labour and in implementing the lisbon objectives. the resolution warns that 'managing immigration does not mean failing to address the reforms of the eu labour markets ... which are necessary to increase labour market integration capacity and the overall ability to adapt to economic change'. the aim is to over-exploit immigrants so that flexible forms of employment and the reduction of wage costs in favour of the overall monopolies can more easily be integrated into the eu's working system and the coercive measures are more easily accepted by people under the pretext that it is a matter of fighting illegal immigration. for these reasons the communist party of greece did not support the resolution.
in the light of the recent events in the middle east, the commission has decided to take the necessary steps to ensure that the situation in the region is not further aggravated by the threat of nuclear weapons. nothing is sacred to the imperialists of the eu. they have no compassion for human suffering and misery. even though we know this, we cannot help but get angry when we read texts like this, especially when it is signed by all the political groups. in return for the 2.3 million euros the eu is providing (small compared to the 31.3 million us dollars needed for the first three months), it is demanding all sorts of things from iran: compliance with the additional protocol to the non-proliferation treaty, dialogue on respect for human rights, speedier reforms, etc. we refer all those who are lying to themselves about the human face of the european union to paragraph 12 of the resolution, which states that the solidarity shown by the international community is also an expression of support for the forces in iran that are fighting to ensure that the country continues to strive to increase democratization. the resolution also does not fail to promote the "generosity" of the american president. we are the last to support the iranian regime. but solidarity should be in the direction that the iranian people want, and not in the direction that the imperialists want.

separate the issues and resolve what could be resolved and not to try to solve everything by paralysing everything. because we all know very well that one way to keep anything from moving is to pretend to achieve perfection overnight. i think that what we have achieved today is very important, and that it is precisely this mixture of vision for the construction of a single european sky and realism which has guided the action of parliament, the commission and - i mean - the council, which allows us today to congratulate ourselves on a positive result, which is a very important step, which we all know will be followed by other steps, but which is the first step in this new frontier of the single european sky.

mr president, we are faced with a new definition of priorities and objectives. the white paper clearly speaks of a demand-driven space policy, and both the white paper and previous texts and official statements make clear the intention to use space for military purposes. some say that space systems are dual-use anyway, civilian and military. i would just point out that it is a political decision to use one or the other. the fact that there are weapons capable of destroying the whole world, for example, does not mean that we will allow them to be used. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i will ask them to reduce their devotion to free markets and competition policy and i will propose public funds for space research and exploration. i would also warn of the anger of the popular movement if it were attempted to use space for political defence and security, a purely aggressive policy aimed at turning the european union into a second world policeman alongside the united states of america. the new orientation can serve business interests, which will benefit both from market mechanisms and from state resources for military applications. but it serves no one else. on the contrary, the european union's entry into the military use of outer space is a serious escalation in the arms race, extremely dangerous for world peace. it is truly a pity that man's most advanced achievements are being used against him. but this is an inevitable feature of the capitalist system. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
