rapporteur. – mr president, the report i have the honour of presenting is an own-initiative report on credit rating agencies, which are business organisations of good standing in the markets. however, their reputation has been shaken somewhat by the recent spate of financial scandals, starting with enron and ending with parmalat. the parmalat fraud is especially relevant as it concerns bondholders: precisely the section of the market that rating agencies are supposed to protect. until a few days before the crash of these companies, they were unable to do anything about protecting the public. these failures have prompted debates in the united states, investigations in the us congress, the us securities and exchange commission, the international organization of securities commission and other international fora. there is a specific european angle on this matter. rating agencies that are one hundred years old in the united states are relative newcomers in europe. in some cases they have given indications of not understanding european business mentality. they have created friction, especially in germany. the other point of special european interest is the inevitable remark that whatever the nature of the regulation of the agencies – however mild and indirect – it has its seat exclusively in the united states, in the form of the securities and exchange commission. the fact that nothing similar exists in europe makes for an unbalanced transatlantic relationship which might become troublesome if it is not redressed in time. these considerations prompted the original proposal of the report in favour of the formation of a european registration authority to be considered by the commission, an authority to which agencies would report on their activities and with which they could establish a regulatory dialogue, a dialogue of equals based on the european security ratings committee, on topics of mutual interest – excluding, of course, any intervention in the substance of the opinions issued by the agencies. it is interesting that the securities and exchange commission has recently expressed interest in making contact with the csr on this matter. the majority of the committee on economic and monetary affairs found this proposal too avant-garde and recommended re-examining the whole area of operations of rating agencies without including in their recommendation anything as specific as the creation of a european registration authority. with all due respect for the majority opinion of my colleagues, i beg to differ for two reasons. firstly, because a regulatory authority operates in the united states and, secondly, because – as i have already said – the lack of a corresponding authority in europe creates a potentially harmful imbalance. i have therefore asked my political group to help me out by resubmitting the proposal for a european registration agency in the form of an amendment, and i urge all colleagues who originally may have differed on this point to think again. this is not a party political issue, but it is certainly a political issue because it involves the interests of europe as a whole. the alternative is that the agencies will eventually – such is the strength of feeling in favour of some regulation of their activities – be forced to report to 15 or 25 national financial authorities in europe, something the agencies themselves must view with horror. rating agencies must continue to do good work. we have proposed no heavy-handed regulation, but, without a european registration agency, we will be failing to attain what is obviously our main objective. while thanking my colleagues for supporting various other useful proposals, i urge them to think again about this.
the commission's proposals on working time, mr president, are in line with the employers' demands for flexibility in working time. they increase the intensity of the exploitation of the working class and maximize the profits of big capital. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. the basic rights of workers, such as the fixed daily working hours, the eight- or seven-hour days, the five-day week, are thus abolished. collective agreements are concluded and fixedterm contracts are extended. the right to insurance and regular leave is being destroyed, and further flexibility in employment relations is being promoted. any positive arrangements are overridden by the exemptions provided for and the application of the 'opting-out' clause imposed by the united kingdom and used by other countries. it is the one that drags workers under pressure to secure a job. this is a system of modern wage slavery, which forces a large proportion of the workforce to work an average of 48 to 70 hours a week, with deteriorating safety and health conditions and very serious consequences for the mental and physical health of workers. we support the reduction of working hours, the introduction of a genuine 35-hour day with a fixed seven-hour working day, five days a week, an increase in wages and full social security and labour rights that will apply universally to all workers and all businesses. we are fighting with the workers to overturn the current anti-working law framework and flexible forms of employment.
mr president, commissioner, once again we are faced with a new threat to public health. this disease shows that communicable diseases go beyond the member states and require immediate measures to prevent and protect the health of european citizens. on 1 february 2004 the world health organisation pointed out the risk of human-to-human transmission of the avian influenza virus. so far, 19 people have died, 23 have been infected, and the virus has appeared in 10 asian countries. the microbe is highly contagious, but the delay in the information once again helped it to spread. the european commission may have underestimated the risk, considering that the microbe is not transmitted from person to person. but we do know that influenza viruses are genetically unstable and their behavior cannot be predicted. so this particular epidemic can change rapidly and continuously. i certainly welcome the measures taken to ban for six months imports of meat and poultry and imports of live birds as pets from asian countries. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. as you well know, the microbe is also carried by free-ranging birds during their migration. we need the expertise of the international food agency so that appropriate guidance can be given and the importance of preventive vaccination stressed wherever possible. history has taught us that in cases where a disease has an economic impact on the agricultural sector but can also harm human health, the risk to humans should not be underestimated but should be a priority if we really want to maintain consumer confidence. i do not think that has happened so far since the measures announced. finally, commissioner, we must be sure that farmers in the affected areas are able to have the appropriate means to incinerate animals infected with the disease without risk of spreading it.
commission. mr president, i have been asked a number of questions and i will endeavour to answer as many as possible. first, mrs jackson asks whether i am satisfied with the inspection services that we have, particularly in relation to our work in asia. in relation to the fvo, i should say that i have not sent the fvo to thailand in respect of this particular outbreak. the experts who are there, who have been offered by me, have been supplying their services through the world health organisation and are in place working with the oie in situ, in vietnam particularly. the fvo will be involved in this exercise only when it is believed there is something profitable to be gained from it, that is in circumstances where there is the possibility of lifting the ban. i have made it clear on a number of occasions publicly that it is not my intention to lift this ban on the importation of thai chicken until such time as there is an independent evaluation of the situation there, in view of the history that a number of you have referred to. the evaluation will be made as a result of a visit from the inspectors of the fvo and following a report from them as to the situation. mrs jackson also asked me whether, in view of the likelihood of concealment, we are getting good information and whether we have a proper rapid response team in place. as i indicated in my earlier remarks, there is in existence an early warning response system. this system is in place, working from luxembourg, and has been in operation for some time. you will remember when i reported to parliament last year on the sars issue, i also pointed to the work of this committee. there is a communicable diseases network which also works in conjunction with the relevant authorities in the member states. i am absolutely satisfied that the system of rapid response that we have in relation to animal and human diseases is very effective. you will remember the very quick response of the commission and my services to the outbreak of foot and mouth disease some years ago, when the commission responded within a matter of hours and all the relevant authorities and member states knew within a matter of hours that there had been this outbreak in the united kingdom. the same is true of public health because we have this system in place. mention was also made of the next item on parliament's agenda: the legislation establishing the european centre for disease prevention and control. i can assure parliament that the evidence is there that the commission and other institutions of the european union are working well to respond quickly to these kinds of issues and to give proper information to the public and, indeed, to respond well and appropriately to outbreaks of this kind. on the question of food labelling, there is community law in place relating to labelling. it may need upgrading and i am looking at that. the legislation in place requires country of origin labelling and certification and stamps from countries such as thailand. so, if cooked chicken from thailand comes pre-packed it has a stamp on it which indicates that and the label also states the country of origin which, as i indicated in this case, is thailand. mrs roth-behrendt asked me if we are doing enough in respect of avian flu and, in particular, blocking food products from asia. it is important to emphasise that we do not trade in any poultry products with any country in asia other than thailand, so the responses given relate to that country. of course we are concerned about the outbreak of animal and bird diseases in other parts of the world and we make sure that we respond properly to those issues. as a number of people mentioned during this debate, we must not lose sight of the fact that we have had these outbreaks of avian influenza in the european union also. we had an outbreak in italy two or three years ago; we had an outbreak in the netherlands last year; there have been outbreaks in belgium and germany too. mrs roth-behrendt also asked me what i am doing about imports from china. it is public knowledge that we banned imports from china some years ago; there was a lot of publicity about that at the time. there are a number of food products we do not import from china, including poultry. on the question of research into vaccinations, this is being done – dg research does have a research programme on food safety. on this same subject which mrs roth-behrendt and others referred to, an informal meeting of the health ministers of the eu and accession countries will be taking place in brussels on thursday evening. one of the items on the agenda is the question of the stockpiling of vaccines in respect of diseases of this kind, of which flu is obviously one. we had a discussion in parliament last year on appropriate responses to the bioterrorism issue, such as stockpiling vaccines against smallpox. there is ongoing work in relation to that also. however, i must emphasise, as mr liese has said, that the competences available to us at european union level in respect of public health issues are quite limited. an attempt was made to extend these competences in the european convention and we made some progress. i still have hopes that we might ultimately make more progress on this before the finalisation of the constitution. mr mulder asked me about the inspection systems. in his opinion inspections are not carried out at the same level for products coming in from outside the european union. under commission decision 2002/995/ec, all personal imports of meat in the luggage of passengers from asia have been banned with the exception of small amounts of specialist food products, such as baby food. under these rules, poultry meat and meat products carried by passengers cannot be introduced into the eu without conforming to the same health requirements as commercial imports. as i said earlier, no commercial imports of poultry meat are allowed from any asian country where the disease is present. mr graefe zu baringdorf then asked me whether i reacted quickly or only when the press publicised this issue. as has been indicated by a number of other speakers, i was in asia when this outbreak became public. i had been in vietnam, from where we do not import any poultry meat, so the issue does not arise there. but i had also been in thailand. it is public knowledge that in the early part of the week when i was there, there was some lack of transparency, if could express it in that way. i was concerned during the couple of days i was there at the mounting circumstantial evidence tending in the other direction. following pressing questions from me, it was made clear to me two or three days later that in fact avian flu was present, both in the human and bird populations. i am satisfied that my presence in thailand helped to improve transparency in relation to this issue. as soon as the presence of avian flu was confirmed to me on 23 january, i put a ban in place within a matter of hours. the ban went in place on that friday at 12 o'clock, brussels time. any suggestion that the commission reacted slowly is incorrect. i cannot imagine any greater degree of surveillance or monitoring than the commissioner being there himself, asking questions, not being satisfied with the answers, pressing further, getting the truth and acting within hours. mr gollnisch asked me about the risk of contamination of the united states virus. it is believed, but not yet confirmed, that this virus is a low pathogenic virus and therefore would not require the same degree of response as for the asian influenza from thailand. however, that is not confirmed, we are still looking at it and our response will depend on our information. i assure him that the lifting of the ban will only take place after independent evaluation of the circumstances. mr fiori asked me about pet birds and other birds. a ban has been put in place in respect of the importation of pet birds. he asked me about coordinating groups. there is a regulatory committee of the eu, which includes member states' experts, who deal with that. he asked me about links between globalisation and diseases. the oie in paris continues evaluating the situation. the commission is a member of that, member states are involved. they are meeting later this month in relation to this issue. the sps agreement is also a relevant contributor to the protection of animal and public health in relation to the wto issues. mr de roo asked me about borders. once again, i refer back to the lack of competences that we have in relation to borders and communicable diseases, but i hope more progress will be made on this. i refer him also to my earlier answer that the health ministers are meeting later this week to deal with many of the issues that he raised about the public health aspect and in particular vaccinations. mr parish said that it was only when i returned that i discovered there was a problem and that i reacted well. as i indicated earlier, that was not the case; i reacted while i was there and the commission responded well to this issue. we have not banned cooked meats for the reasons that have been stated. i am advised that cooked meats above 60 degrees – and we put in a safety margin of another 10 degrees – are safe for human consumption. the inspectors in the european union, and in the member states themselves, know what is necessary and carry out appropriate inspections to ensure that there are no breaches of community law in relation to this issue. he also referred to labelling and i refer him to my earlier answer. mrs oomen-ruijten asked me whether we followed the member states' lead in this. the actual chronology of events is that on 23 january the commission put in place the ban in circumstances i have already outlined; member states responded with the regulatory committee confirming that ban on 6 february; and a further ban was put in place in respect of feathers – mr nicholson asked me about that – on 28 january. mr sturdy asked me how long it took for the ban to be put in place. i have already answered that question. there were a number of questions in relation to the number of inspectors at the border posts. this is a member state competence. the member states themselves make sure that they have enough officials to ensure that the importation is properly inspected. the food and veterinary office inspectors monitor this when they carry out their inspections. they report, and the reports are public. there are no reports of any concerns in relation to this. on labelling, i refer you to my earlier answer. concerning feathers, again i refer you to my earlier answer: that ban has been in place since 28 january. mrs cardoso made reference to the vulnerability of pigs and i would confirm that this is a concern and is being watched very closely. there have been reports of some infection found in the noses of some pigs in vietnam, but it has not extended any further and there has been no evidence of mutation of the flu virus. this is, however, being watched very closely because of the similarity between the immune system of pigs and of humans. therefore, as she rightly points out, it is of considerable concern. mr wyn asked me whether the health authorities are doing everything that they can to stop the spread of this disease. i am impressed by the work undertaken by my own staff. i have had detailed consultations and briefings and i am satisfied that their own work, their interaction with the who, the fao and the oie, and also their interaction with the experts in the member states, indicate to me that this issue is being taken very seriously, that the officials, at both national and international level, understand the seriousness of this and that they are doing everything in their power to ensure that the disease is not spread. one of the difficulties being faced in some poorer areas is that the stamping-out of the disease is best achieved by killing the birds in question. this is of course a matter of concern in poor countries, because unless the authorities involved face up to the question of compensation there is a risk that a disease of this kind may be hidden. that may be a contributing factor to the earlier lack of full transparency about this issue in that part of the world. this has to be faced and we have to emphasise it in our relations with the authorities in member states. these kinds of diseases cannot be hidden, their presence will come out, will be known, and the consequences ultimately will be worse for those countries who engage in that exercise, because they undermine the confidence of the authorities engaged in food safety, like ours in the european union. the stringent measures necessary before the ban is lifted in thailand, with the requirement that there will be independent evaluation, will be particularly necessary in this instance because of the lack of transparency at an earlier stage. we must be assured that our own authorities are satisfied, which is why no lifting of the ban in thailand will take place until the fvo has carried out an inspection on the ground and reported back that things are satisfactory.
commission. mr president, we are watching the situation in vietnam very carefully and in particular the spread of this disease there. there may be a risk that the outbreak of the disease there is more serious and more widespread than in thailand. i believe that to be the case. i must emphasise that we do not import any poultry from vietnam, so there is no risk to public health. there is no risk to animal health from direct importation from vietnam and therefore our concern in this relates to ensuring the containment of the spread of the disease in asia itself as a matter of general interest, but it is not related to any further risk as a result of any importation of birds, because we do not engage in that kind of trade with vietnam.
commission. i know that mr mulder is interested in this matter and that he has had direct contact with my cabinet about it as recently as in the last few days. the items he mentions as requiring financing are being financed. the question is: under what heading will they be dealt with? if it is believed to be necessary to transfer from one budget heading to another and that this is the best way of dealing with it, then this can be done. this matter is under review. however, it is important to emphasise that the items he mentions as requiring financing are not being under-financed. they are being financed. whatever refinement may be necessary in the future can be dealt with at that time.

- the next item is the report (a5-0038/2004) by mr bowis, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, on the proposal for a regulation of the european parliament and of the council establishing a european centre for disease prevention and control (com (2003) 441 - c5-0400/2003 - 2003/0174 (cod)).
mr president, commissioner, recent data in the health sector have shown that a single member state, even if it has all the necessary means, cannot effectively tackle all the threats. the spread of epidemics is facilitated by population movements, environmental and social changes, globalisation, wars. the community, alongside its obligations under the treaties, must also respond to the concerns of citizens. this is why the creation of a centre focused on early diagnosis, assessment and communication of existing and emerging public health risks in a rapid, flexible and effective way is more than necessary. the european centre for disease prevention and control will coordinate and support the work of the european commission, the member states and the epidemiological monitoring and control network for communicable diseases. it will work in conjunction with all existing public health services, such as the world health organisation and the european food safety authority. on behalf of my group, mr president, and in the light of recent events - and i refer to the previous debate we had on avian influenza - i would like to stress that we agreed with the rapporteur's proposal to close the matter at first reading and to have the centre operational in 2005, even if we are not entirely satisfied with the council's positions. i would like to thank the rapporteur for accepting my suggestions to extend the scope of the regulation to other serious health threats, not just communicable diseases. i would also like to thank him for accepting the need to record and properly manage the impact of recent epidemics not only on physical but also on mental health. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the establishment of a european agency for the evaluation of medicinal products for human use is a very good one. in conclusion, i would like to congratulate and thank the rapporteur once again.
mr president, today is an important day because, after many years of discussions and findings on the need to strengthen the union's capacity for timely and coordinated action on threats to public health, we are finally discussing the establishment of the european centre for disease prevention and control. in the last 20 years, there have been 30 new infectious diseases or variants of old ones, due to mutations, viruses, or even old diseases that are resurfacing, like tuberculosis, to give one example. all these public health crises, as well as the most recent ones of sars and avian influenza, have highlighted the weaknesses and gaps in existing structures. i would like to believe that the centre, with a small but adequate scientific organisational structure, will be able to fill these gaps effectively. however, in order to be effective and efficient, it must be the central point of reference and coordination for all the national monitoring centres and reference laboratories in the member states. i would like to thank the rapporteur because not only did he accept the amendments i tabled, but then, in conjunction with them, two very important points were adopted and accepted, which were not included in the original proposal. the first concerns the development of a strategy for vaccination, which is a very important weapon in the fight against the challenge of infectious diseases. the second concerns the identification of current public health issues and the promotion of relevant applied research, in order to strengthen our preventive capacity and to promote the effectiveness of the coordinated actions undertaken. mr president, i hope that we will overcome the last hesitation and that the centre will soon become a reality, albeit in the relatively limited form in which it appears today.
commission. mr president, this interesting debate highlights the urgency and importance of this proposal. the political pressure to have the centre set up and running as soon as possible reflects the needs and concerns of european citizens. for the europe of today, where millions of people cross national borders every day, we need to be in a position to take rapid, coordinated action at eu level to best protect the health of our citizens. this is precisely what the centre aims to achieve. by pooling our scientific expertise and reinforcing our rapid alert systems, the ecdc will allow better preparation and swifter and more effective responses to disease outbreaks. in summing up this morning's debate, i would like to focus on three central issues in the proposal which have been key areas of concern: the mission and the tasks of the centre; the possible extension of its sphere of operation; and the composition of the management board. there is broad agreement between the institutions on the initial scope of the centre. we all agree that it should start with communicable diseases and be able to respond to cases of unexpected outbreaks of unknown origin. the council and parliament share the view that there needs to be an external evaluation to assess the feasibility of extending the scope based on the performance and impact of the centre, as resources permit. clearly, the budget for the centre will dictate its expansion, and beyond 2006 this has to be decided within the new financial perspectives. to enable the centre to start, and for it to make an impact, the commission is prepared to accept this approach, but it means that when the question of the need to extend its remit arises we should – and would – expect parliament and the council to address such a proposal rapidly, under the codecision procedure. as regards the organisational aspects of the centre, the proposal provides for responsibility for the day-to-day operation of the centre to be granted to the director. the advisory forum is the place for the director to seek advice from his or her peers – and the forum would ensure the shared ownership of this venture with the member states. the commission can accept the extension of the membership of the advisory forum to stakeholders such as non-governmental organisations and academics. it will be an advantage for the director to have broader views when developing the operation of the centre. the management board should steer policy and make decisions. the commission’s proposal balances this dual responsibility in a way which we consider to be the most efficient and transparent. this approach is based on the principles defined in the commission communication on the governance of european agencies. in a resolution adopted last month, parliament welcomed this communication, recognising the need to limit the size of the boards of the agencies. the commission wants to follow a consistent approach on this matter, as currently each agency seems to follow a different path. i now note that the council and parliament have reached agreement on a board for the ecdc to consist of one representative per member state, three from the commission and two from parliament. the commission considers that a board of 30 members is unwieldy and may hamper the decision-making process. although, given the competences involved and being exercised here, i agree with the rapporteur that a good case can be made for one member per member state being on the board. the commission notes, however, the terms of the compromise supported by council and parliament and is therefore willing, despite our concerns, to go along with this compromise, given the importance that it attaches to the rapid establishment of the centre. the commission notes that the evaluation provided for in article 31 of the regulation will cover the working practises of the centre, including the composition and functioning of the management board. a full listing of the commission’s position on each of the amendments is being made available to parliament commission's position on amendments by parliament: see annex.. in short, this states that the commission accepts all amendments that are part of the compromise package as set out by the rapporteur. i also join with the rapporteur in urging that amendments outside the package should not – and cannot – be accepted by the commission. i conclude by thanking the rapporteur and parliament. when i formulated this idea in my mind some years ago, i not only got the support that i would expect from my own services, but also very strong support from parliament. we should be proud of ourselves today. by voting tomorrow on this issue we are reacting in a timely way in circumstances where we see communicable diseases on a global basis. we have discussed it in the last debate here in parliament. we can proudly say to our citizens that we are responding well and quickly to their needs in relation to issues associated with communicable disease. sometimes we can be unduly pessimistic and critical of ourselves. this is one occasion where we can stand up and say that we have done our work well and quickly, we have responded to the needs, and we can look our citizens in the eye and tell them that we have done our work well.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place at 11.30 a.m. today.

(es) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european maritime safety agency has not been in operation for very long. however, the commission has proposed to extend its functions in three very specific and topical areas: the fight against pollution caused by ships, the training of crews and marine protection. i would point out that the recent maritime disasters have made it clear that new measures are needed at european level, not only in the prevention of pollution but also in the provision and management of resources to combat pollution when it occurs. the agency's action in the field of pollution control will complement the provisions in force in the member states and add value to the actions already undertaken at community level in the field of civil protection. it will complement the intervention systems of the member states and will in no way replace them - nor is it intended to do so. for this purpose, the agency may charter specialised and multi-purpose vessels. in addition to providing the appropriate means, the agency shall provide technical and scientific support to the union states and the commission in this field. as regards the minimum level of training for seafarers, our proposal is simply a comparison with the existing provision in the regulation of the agency, with a more important role for the community, under directive 2001/25/ec. it is therefore invited to contribute actively to the new procedures for the recognition of seafarers' certificates of competency issued in third countries. in the field of civil protection too, our proposal seeks to take account of the new community competence for improving the safety of ships and port facilities. the regulation introducing this new competence will enter into force shortly, thanks to the agreement reached by parliament and the council at first reading. the agreement requires the commission to check the effectiveness of the control procedures introduced by the member states. the agency's assistance is fully indicated in the conduct of these inspections. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. although this approach is more restrictive than the one we have advocated, the commission could accept it. in conclusion, mr president, mr president, mr robles' recommendation was unanimously agreed by the members of the committee, and we therefore encourage him to go further. the system of liability under the two international conventions is multi-faceted. in addition to the subjective liability, which does not work in practice, there is the objective liability of the shipowner, with a maximum compensation of eur 72 million, and the additional liability from the fund in the event of the shipowner's failure or exceeding this amount, which amounts after the amendment of 92 to eur 250 million. the extensive damage caused by the errika and prestige accidents demonstrated the inadequacy of the compensation provided. in december 2000, an attempt was made, on the basis of a commission proposal, to set up a european supplementary fund for damage to european waters. this liability would also be additional because the obligation to compensate would apply if there were no compensation from the funds. although this proposal was approved by the european parliament, it was not accepted by the council. it is a happy coincidence that we know that in may 2003 the protocol was adopted to establish an international compensation fund with a maximum compensation of eur 1 billion. it is again a supplementary fund. in the absence of community rules on compensation for oil pollution damage, the council's decision to accede to the protocol must be regarded by all of us as a very positive step. the european parliament must therefore give its unreserved assent on the legal basis of article 200 (3) of the treaty. it should be noted that there is exclusive competence in matters regulated by the protocol on the supplementary fund. however, there is no conflict with regulation no 44/2001 concerning jurisdiction, recognition and enforcement of judgments, since the protocol is based on the existing regime of the 1992 convention. it is clarified that this is not an accession of the european union itself but an authorization to the member states to ratify the protocol. the rapporteur rightly points out that this must be done as soon as possible, even before june of this year. we hope that in future negotiations the community will be able to become a party to the protocol, something which is not currently provided for in the protocol.

mr. president, iran is celebrating 25 years of the islamic revolution amidst a deep political crisis, with thousands of progressive candidates barred from the upcoming elections and with millions of iranians who voted in favor of reform wondering where the country is headed. the extent of the exclusions also includes several prominent women who spearheaded legislative reforms and this has resulted in further deterioration of the position of women in iran. these are women with whom we have worked constructively in the women's committee of our parliament. mr president, i would like to express my surprise and at the same time my anger at the situation we are facing with the four greek and three filipino seamen on board the tanker tasman spirit. this tanker ran aground last summer outside the port of karachi in pakistan because of a mistake by the pakistani pilot who was driving it into the port. the sea level was very low due to the tidal phenomenon, so there was a mistake on the part of the pakistani authorities and we had a dramatic event. of course, many tons of oil were spilled into the sea but beyond that the handling by the pakistani authorities was incomprehensible. the greek and filipino sailors were the only victims of this unfortunate incident. the question has not been dealt with in a comprehensive way so that we can see what the responsibilities of the company, the pakistani pilot, etc. are. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the situation in the gulf is not worsened, and that the situation in the gulf is not worsened. besides, we had something else: the head of the rescue crew, a greek sailor, went to help and is also being held by pakistani authorities. the situation is without a doubt tragic. i have personally met the families of seafarers who are frankly in a desperate state and that is why parliament must react, i believe it will react, because it is a question of humanity above all.
mr president, apart from what mr hadjidakis said, there is a relatively recent report on the circumstances of the shipwreck by the competent pakistani ministry, which does not contain any evidence that would even remotely support the charge against the seamen. also, the ship's insurers have long submitted proposals for compensation without receiving any official response from the pakistani authorities, and have spent over 20 million dollars on this accident, on the shipwreck's transportation, etc. we know that next week, on the 18th, the european union troika with commissioner patten will be going to islamabad and that they have managed to put this issue at the top of the agenda for eu-pakistan relations. we hope that this debate, which has long been a torment to our emotions and our reason, but above all to innocent marine biologists, will bear fruit. we note that there have been interventions by the greek foreign ministry, by george papandreou as foreign minister and by the deputy minister, without any substantive response from his pakistani counterpart. there's a terrible situation. in addition to what mr hadjidakis said about the greek sailor who tried to commit suicide, there is the following comic or tragic fact: attempted suicide is a criminal offence in greece. so now this man is on trial, this wreck, for attempting suicide. we hope it won't be a criminal offence to commit suicide when it happens. with such attitudes, as you can see, who will turn qualified young people to maritime professions, who will ultimately protect the lives of seafarers and the marine environment tomorrow? we are asking, mr president, that humanity, reason and legality prevail in this case at last and we are relying very much on the efforts of commissioner patten in islamabad the day after tomorrow.
mr president, i too would like to add a few words on behalf of the members of the communist party of greece and the whole of the confederal group of the european united left who, together with other groups, have signed and supported the joint resolution calling for the immediate release of the crew of the tasman spirit illegally detained since july 2003 in karachi by the pakistani authorities. this is a clear case of hostage taking for financial gain. it is significant that the crew is also being held in pakistan along with the engineer mr pappas, who arrived in karachi 20 days after the ship was docked as the head of the rescue operation, which was also successful. it is inconceivable that the entire crew should be detained because of the ecological disaster caused by the ship's mooring at the entrance to karachi harbour and that the ship's cook should be held responsible. moreover, the anchorage took place when the vessel was under the responsibility of the port authorities of the local shipowner. the situation is particularly worrying for the health of seamen who are detained and, without any responsibility, are prevented from returning home, which has even led to suicide attempts. we must stress once again that we cannot continue this unacceptable tactic which is being used by many governments and which is in the interests of ship owners and insurance companies, namely to use ship owners as scapegoats for resolving economic disputes and to shoulder responsibility for what happens at sea which they do not have. we call on the european commission, and in particular the presidency and the greek government, to intervene decisively and to demand the immediate release of the seamen detained in karachi. their hostage-taking cannot be prolonged and must end immediately.
mr. president, in an effort to end the conflicts plaguing the peloponnese, the oracle of delphi advised the warring parties in 776 b.c. to organize games, putting aside their differences, so that athletes and citizens from all over greece could participate in these games in safety. thus was born the ancient greek tradition of the olympic truce, which was observed for more than 1,000 years. the revival of this institution was promoted by greece, with the support of the un, the european council, unesco and the international olympic committee. in view of the 2004 olympic games in greece, the international olympic truce center was established and undertook a major campaign. over 400 personalities from around the world have co-signed the olympic truce text. the olympic games, which return this year to their home country and to europe, are a global reference point and can send, along with the message of sporting achievement, the message of a culture of peace. peace and dialogue are two objectives which the european parliament has always supported. these are values that we continue to put at the heart of our efforts, no matter how much time and effort it takes to achieve them. each of us, the members of the european parliament, is called upon to become an ambassador of goodwill for the olympic truce, promoting the message and culture of peace at all meetings, missions, interparliamentary committees and international forums in every corner of the globe where we frequently find ourselves. in this endeavour we look to the council and the irish presidency for their support and assistance. the central slogan of the olympic truce is: "if we can have peace for 16 days, maybe we can have it forever".

mr president, on behalf of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities, which i have the honour of chairing, i thank you for your statement on the occasion of international women's day and for the emphasis you and other colleagues have placed on the need for a balanced representation of women and men in the european parliament, which will be elected in june. however, mr president, i have already received a very serious complaint from the women's organizations of malta, who inform us that the leaders of the political parties have already drawn up their lists without including a single woman! i wonder, mr president, whether the population of malta is composed only of men and not of women. i would ask you, mr president, on the occasion of today's international women's day, to intervene personally in this matter and to remind the political leaders of malta that they must respect the principle of gender equality and the european acquis, otherwise their position in the european union is likely to be very problematic.
mr president, it is a great honour for me to address the house on the occasion of international women's day in the context of the oral questions procedure. mr president, today's international women's day, which coincides with the 20th anniversary of the existence and achievements of the women's committee of our parliament, is a very good opportunity to take stock of and evaluate our policy on gender equality. there is no doubt that in the last twenty years there has been considerable progress in all areas thanks to the political initiatives taken at community and national level, as well as the initiatives taken by the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities of the european parliament, and i would like to take this opportunity to thank president cox for his support. the participation of women in the european parliament increased from 6% of the appointed parliament to 16% in 1979, when the first elections were held, and to 31% in 1999. we want and hope that this percentage will be further increased in the next elections to the enlarged europe of 25. gender equality and the full exploitation of women's productive potential are now widely recognised as a necessary condition for the success of the lisbon strategy and for the strengthening of our participatory democracy. of course, the greatest progress in the last 20 years has been in the participation and performance of women at all levels of education, as they now represent 55% of graduates in the european union. however, despite the undoubted achievements, significant gender differences and disparities still exist in all areas, as the first annual report of the commission, which will be discussed at the spring european council in a few days, shows. violence against women, trafficking in human beings, unemployment, incompatibility of work and private life, inadequate childcare and elderly care infrastructure, and inequality in pay and promotion are pressing problems that urgently need to be addressed, as they are directly linked to the violation of rights and to the viability and competitiveness of the economy. unfortunately, we are concerned and saddened to note that many member states, despite having incorporated all the community directives into their national legislation, are not willing to promote accompanying measures that will lead to the elimination of inequalities. it is disappointing that many member states do not even follow the council's recommendations. looking back over the last five years, the revision of the equal treatment directive in the labour market and the promotion of gender mainstreaming have been important steps in promoting equal opportunities. however, because it is not possible to speak of gender equality only in the workplace, we welcomed the initiative of mrs diamantopoulou for a new directive based on article 13 of the treaty, which would prohibit any discrimination in access to goods and services. it is a fact that with our joint efforts and within the framework of the future european constitution we can gradually bridge the gender gap in the union and act as a shining example for the rest of the world, for regions of the world where women are still fighting for their fundamental human rights. as we approach the 10th anniversary of the 1995 un world conference in beijing, where 180 governments agreed on a common platform for action to promote women's rights, we see that those commitments have not been met. women still represent 70% of the world's poor, girls in sub-saharan africa are six times more likely than boys to be infected with hiv, four million women and children are victims of violence and sexual exploitation; the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. in the context of the new challenges created by technological development and economic globalisation, we therefore need to renew and intensify action on women's rights, with a view to democratic and participatory global governance that ensures a fair distribution of the planet's resources, gender equality and sustainable development for all. we can discuss these in a new conference, which we will call 'beijing + 10', and for which we await an initiative from the commission. i would ask you, commissioner, to answer the following questions: firstly, what are the concrete results of the work of the group of commissioners on promoting equality, secondly, whether the commission agrees to appoint a commissioner who will have gender equality as the main issue in his/her portfolio and also there are no council representatives here; we have asked the council to ask the member states to submit a list of three candidates for commissioners representing both sexes, in order to achieve a gender balanced composition of the new commission. the commission has already submitted this proposal to the assembly.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: mr president, commissioner, it is my honour to present the very important report on the situation of women belonging to minority groups in the european union on behalf of the rapporteur, mrs elena valenciano, who is unable to be present at today's sitting. the report refers to three minority groups: women with disabilities, women immigrants and roma women, and focuses on the problem of the veil of silence covering their situation. these women remain in the shadows, hiding behind the male members of their group. in order to make the analysis more accurate, the rapporteur proposes various actions for the minority groups mentioned in the report, such as the development of statistics and the carrying out of a specific study, the participation of these women in decision-making processes, access to information, training and education, the protection of their sexual and reproductive health. the rapporteur thanks the organisations of these three minorities for their support to women and for the good cooperation they have had in the preparation of this report. firstly, as regards women with disabilities, there are 44 600 000 people with disabilities in the european union, representing 15.7% of european citizens and expected to increase to 25% with the enlargement of the european union. it is estimated that women account for 51% of the total disabled population. however, there is evidence that this percentage may increase by 2015, to 53% and possibly 58%. compared to 55% of able-bodied women workers, only 25% of women with disabilities are employed. the jobs they do are usually low-level, poorly paid and with fewer benefits than those enjoyed by men with disabilities. the problem is that there is no vocational guidance designed to meet the specific needs of women with disabilities. violence is not just a common phenomenon in the lives of these women, but in many cases it is the very cause of their disability. it is estimated that violence is the leading cause of death and disability worldwide for women between the ages of 16 and 45, over cancer, road accidents, and even war. secondly, as regards migrant women, according to the un's overall figures, since 1990 (unfortunately we do not have any more recent figures) there are 12 million migrant women in europe. this number is certainly still increasing, although precise figures are not available. poverty and social instability are central factors in women's lives. difficulties in accessing the labour market as a result of cultural and language barriers, lack of recognition of their professional qualifications, stereotypes and prejudices, racism and indifference to their rights restrict migrant women to the lower levels of the labour market or the black economy or make them dependent on men for their survival. many of these women remain at home and work as housewives and cleaners, clinging to the traditional female role, resulting in social isolation and lack of access to basic social infrastructure, but also marginalization in the process of social mobility, a situation that has ripple effects of social exclusion. closing borders and restrictions on immigration increase the role and profits of informal and mafia-like networks involved in human trafficking. in the case of female victims of trafficking in human beings, mainly for the purpose of sexual exploitation, the issue of temporary residence permits should be encouraged. as regards roma women, there are an estimated 8 to 10 million roma in europe, although the traditionally nomadic nature of this people makes the available data unreliable. so we have to look at these figures very carefully. the census puts roma women at 51%, a proportion that corresponds to the general proportion of women. there is a high birth rate among these populations, as well as high infant and adult mortality rates. women usually marry very young and have their first child earlier than in other ethnic groups. life expectancy is 13 to 17 years shorter for roma (men and women) compared to the rest of the population. the high birth rates combined with the high mortality rates form an extremely young roma population. 80% are under 34 years of age, while 43% are under 14 years of age. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done.
the rapporteur. madam president, this report is the last stage in an important process, a process which began with the report by my dear colleague and friend, jo leinen, a process which reached its peak during the greek presidency, when the presidency and the council, after exchanging views with parliament, finally adopted regulation 2003/2004, which substantially improves the status and standing of european political parties in the european arena. i do not think anyone would disagree with the view that we must do everything possible to ensure that the european political parties, an institution which complements and completes the framework of european democracy, can function properly and promptly. in this context, the report which i am presenting to the house, asking for its support, constitutes a system, a process, which will guarantee european political parties the means by which they can achieve their objectives. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the european parliament is the most important institution in the european union, and it is the only institution in the union that has the power to make decisions. it also takes account of the balance of power and the balance of power within parliament. firstly, the european political parties and their funding, that is to say the provision of the means they need, depends primarily on the bureau of the european parliament, the institution within the european parliament which is responsible for all matters relating to the smooth functioning of the political forces which are active and associated with it. but at the same time the bureau is very pleased to have two more institutions of the european parliament in its efforts to fulfil properly the role assigned to it in relation to european political parties. the first is the conference of presidents of the political groups, which is the most political institution in the european parliament, and the second is the committee on constitutional affairs, the committee of the european parliament responsible for all matters relating to constitutional, constitutional and legal matters. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but in conclusion, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, and by asking the house to vote on my report tomorrow, i take full political responsibility for this recommendation. we have made a very great effort and i would like to thank all my colleagues in all the political groups for their advice and support during the debate on this report in the committee on constitutional affairs. i am therefore grateful to everyone, including you, madam president, and i ask the house to approve this report.

in writing. - (el) we are voting against the dimitrakopoulos report because it completes the procedures for promoting the main idea behind the statute of european parties and their financing, that is to say, the promotion of bodies and mechanisms which 'create a european consciousness' and which foster the view that capitalist anti-people policies are the only way forward. no other opinion may be expressed. steps are taken to silence opponents of the system. rights are abolished, such as freedom of speech and the right to form political parties. on the basis of community requirements and the decisions of capital, the european parliament is made the judge of the 'political correctness' of these parties. the majority will decide whether the minorities are entitled to exist or, at any rate, to put forward their ideas and whether or not funds should be allocated to them. the amendments tabled by mr dimitrakopoulos are moving in a more conservative direction. while the more easily controlled bureau of the european parliament is given too much power, the european parliament itself is given the right to decide only by a "majority of the votes cast" (not even by an absolute majority of its members) whether a party respects the principles of the eu or not, that is, whether it fits into the eu's broad framework and its system. we shall vote against this report because it transforms the european parliament from being a meeting place for different ideas into a judge of the right of ideas to exist.

(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) the proposals in the lavarra report do not mention at all the negative changes that the mid-term review is seeking to bring about also for olive oil, since, as it says, it regards it as a fait accompli, after which only certain aspects of the olive oil market remain to be regulated. that is why all his amendments, almost all of which are positive (such as the abolition of the existing mixtures of olive oil and other fatty substances), are limited to this. we fundamentally disagree with the idea that the mid-term reform of olive oil is definitive and with the content of the reform, i.e. the decoupling of a substantial part of the aid from production, the freezing of olive production at the level of 1 may 1998, the reduction of community funds for olive oil, etc. the commission proposal should not even be the subject of a debate in the european parliament and should be rejected in its entirety, as it creates a situation far worse than the present one, which is by no means satisfactory for the olive growers. we have voted against the report, despite the positive aspects, because it takes the mid-term reform of olive oil for granted. the committee calls on olive growers to fight for the reform not to be implemented in practice, for the removal of the ceilings on olive production and for the payment of aid to actual production, so that olive growers are no longer the object of ridicule and ridicule.

i'm the editor. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the internal market. when we came in the summer of 1999 to take up our new post, we came with the helsinki decision, which marked the unification of the accession process of the new countries, the single wave. today, as we approach the end of our mandate, this cycle is coming to a successful close, and this is something very serious and important for the whole of europe. the two months left are a short time. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in this sense, i, as rapporteur for lithuania, wish to confirm the important positive work that has been produced in this country and has promoted it so that in two months it will be able to become a full member of the european union with equal rights. there are problems, as in all new countries. the commission has also taken steps to ensure that the european parliament's powers are not undermined by the fact that the council has not yet taken a decision on the matter. what are we supposed to do? we must support this effort, we must support environmental issues, we must support the strengthening of transeuropean networks in the field of energy and in the field of transport, so that these countries can be more fully integrated into european structures. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and whether he is prepared to accept them. it closes that circle and it closes in a great way. i hope, on the basis of what the commissioner has told us earlier, that the european union's position on this matter will be firm, so that when cyprus becomes a member of the european union and the cyprus problem is resolved, which we hope will be resolved, the acquis communautaire can be applied 100% to all policies in the whole of the reunified cyprus. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply.
the committee on legal affairs and the internal market has adopted an opinion on the proposal. mr president, the accession of 10 new countries to the european union means mutual recognition, as the rapporteur, mr brok, has pointed out, that beyond the traditional national interest there is a general european interest, which should be regarded by each country as part of its national interest. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the report also shows that the whole process of accession has accelerated the progress of these countries by reforming their economic, administrative and political environment, and this is a great benefit for their peoples. in particular, i would like to express the satisfaction of the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights at the fact that these countries have aligned themselves with the acquis communautaire in the areas of company law, accounting rules, customs law, capital movements and, in general, for the remarkable results they have achieved as a whole in the field of transposition of legislation into their national law. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. it therefore stressed the need to send a clear message that the great prospects open to these peoples are interlinked with their obligations to apply community legislation. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply.
the committee also welcomed the commission's proposal to extend the scope of the directive to include the transport of dangerous goods by road. mr president, i shall now refer to the main points of my report as adopted. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. it calls for the removal of its flag from the paris list to remain a priority for it. it regrets that chapter 21 on regional policy and coordination of the structural funds has not yet been completed. it called for effective structures to be set up to prevent, detect and possibly punish fraud and corruption, particularly in the phare and ispa programmes. it also calls for greater coordination and cooperation between ministries, with relevant services and with other interested parties. it considers that immediate efforts are needed to promote effective cooperation and communication, particularly between central and regional levels, an area where we believe there is a problem, and to consolidate and complete institutional structures, both at central and regional levels. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
commission. – mr president, as we are only a matter of weeks away from enlargement it is most welcome for parliament to be sending a strong signal that it wants the internal market to be strengthened to serve effectively the interests and aspirations of more than 450 million european citizens and well over 20 million companies. i should like to thank the rapporteur, mr miller, and his colleagues in the committee on legal affairs and the internal market for all their hard work in raising the profile of the internal market over the last few years. indeed, they can claim credit for some of the measures in the commission's internal market strategy, as these were originally their ideas. the internal market is one of europe's finest achievements. since 1993 it has helped to create more than 2.5 million extra jobs and has added nearly eur 900 billion, cumulatively, to our collective prosperity. there is much more to come! there are still major gaps in the legal framework, such as on services and taxation. furthermore, in a union of 25 member states or more, we need to strengthen the foundations on which the internal market is built. the internal market strategy 2003-2006 sets out exactly what needs to be done by whom and by when. it is a ten-point plan for making europe better off. there is no time now to go into all the details of parliament's reports or, indeed, of the internal market strategy. therefore, i will focus on a few key issues. i welcome parliament's support for strengthening the mutual recognition principle. i know that views differ on where the balance needs to be struck between mutual recognition and harmonisation. the benefits of mutual recognition are that it respects national traditions and avoids further regulation at the level of the european union. we all know that negotiating harmonising directives will be no sinecure with 25 member states around the table. therefore, if we can avoid more legislation, so much the better. the point, however, is not to impose mutual recognition at all costs. by getting member states to notify cases where mutual recognition cannot be relied upon we shall be much better placed to propose a targeted solution which does not go beyond what is necessary to kick-start free movement again. i share parliament's concern over the slow implementation of internal market directives. late transposition creates legal insecurity and can cause significant harm to businesses and citizens from other member states. it is difficult to understand why member states that fulfil their obligations do not hold to task other member states that are consistently late in doing so. the commission, of course, takes immediate legal action where member states fail to transpose on time. nor do we shy away from hanging member states' dirty linen out to dry. public embarrassment often has more impact than a pile of infringement proceedings in the post. late transposition, however, is only a symptom of a larger problem. the bottom line is that we shall not have a fully functioning internal market as long as member states treat the internal market like a rented car that they do not need to wash or maintain. i am therefore attracted by parliament's suggestion that member states should establish internal market offices that would be responsible for good internal market behaviour and to which people could apply if they have a problem. we shall certainly pursue this line of thinking.
mr president, the main virtue of mr miller's report is that it seeks a global approach. the market strategy is therefore rightly considered to be one of the three instruments for promoting the lisbon and gothenburg objectives, together with the broad economic policy guidelines and the employment guidelines. the proper functioning of the market therefore requires acceptance of this basic policy objective. the lisbon strategy has failed to produce the results we expected, and that is because of the ambiguities about its actual content. we believe that the same way as the competitiveness of the market is proposed, the social dimension of the strategy for it should be considered without fear. competitiveness, in the sense of 'quality competitiveness' rather than 'competitiveness based on a continuous reduction in labour costs', will thus be transformed from a predominant value for economic relations into a general value for the whole of social relations. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information it receives. if this strategic objective is accepted unreservedly, we can more effectively solve the individual problems of the dysfunction of the internal market. the first concern is to convince the member states that the internal market cannot function without further harmonization of their legislation, including the removal of tax barriers, and without increased responsibility for the prompt and unbiased implementation of the agreed rules. the current system of monitoring the application of community rules has failed and needs a radical overhaul. the second concern is to deal with the phenomena of exploitation of the internal market by only a part of enterprises and the working population. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of consumers in the event of a breach of the directive is a very good one. so first of all we have to look at what is it that makes the ordinary citizen resist the internal market and how to link its future to the vision of europe.
in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to finance the construction of the new railway line. the commission's programme for transeuropean networks, however, does not meet the real needs of development for the benefit of workers and the most deprived, the need to raise living standards and the need for easier communication between peoples. on the contrary, it is tying up considerable national resources - in some countries almost all public investment - to projects chosen by big capital, whose only criteria are its own needs, greater profits and the consolidation of its position. the priority projects and areas are those which ensure that private capital can participate in them and then exploit them, whereas these infrastructures should be the property of the people and be designed to meet the needs of the people. there are projects which are not combined with productive activities, projects which are harmful to the environment and projects which contribute little or nothing to the local community. we are opposed to any form of participation by private capital and even more so to the exploitation of projects by private capital. we believe that the workers should demand that infrastructure projects be public, that they should set their own priorities and demand public investment with community participation across the board.
in writing. - (el) there is panic in brussels because the goal set four years ago in lisbon of making the eu the most competitive economy in the world (to the benefit of eu monopolies) is far from being achieved. the council, meeting on 25 and 26 march, is called upon to take even more anti-people measures to regain the ground lost as a result of the reactions and demonstrations of the population. the commission's proposals as a whole, but also for each country, are revealing. for example, greece is accused of not yet putting the elderly to work, not having made fundamental changes to the pension system, not having reduced employers' contributions to pension funds, not having made employment contracts sufficiently flexible, not having sufficiently promoted parttime employment and not having completed privatisations. the national collective agreement is also targeted because it hampers employers who would prefer local employment agreements or individual agreements. these provocative demands must be met by the greek and other governments, and we have no doubt that they will be happy to do so, as they have been for so many years. but there is also the other pole, the workers' movement and the popular movement in general, which has the power to overthrow these plans if it stands together and fights.
in the case of the applicant countries, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the accession countries are able to comply with the accession criteria. in reality, the issue is the integration of these countries into the european union, and that is why the members of the kke in the european parliament will vote against the report, which has every reason to be pleased with the 'progress' which has allegedly been made in these countries. the people have every reason to be concerned. the public enterprises and their wealth sold cheaply to big capital, the abolition of all socialist land and property, the limitation of their sovereign rights, etc. are the price that the people of the candidate countries are paying and will continue to pay. the report is full of hypocrisy in terms of human rights. there is no mention of the lack of political rights of the russian minority in the baltic states, of the persecution and banning of the communist parties, their leaders and their symbols. the eu does not consider this to be a violation of human rights, as its leaders have repeatedly and provocatively stated. in particular, as regards cyprus, we wonder what the reference to the forthcoming inclusion of only greek cypriots is supposed to mean. and why is there pressure to obtain more concessions from the greek cypriot side when everyone knows that denktash is responsible for the failure of the negotiations? we are convinced that the cypriot people will fight for a just and lasting solution.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) the completion of the internal market is a key objective for capital, to secure the greatest possible privileges, to put pressure on small and mediumsized enterprises and to make more efficient use of labour. the expansion of markets is accompanied by the invasion of public services by private capital and the selling off of the property of the people. unrestrained privatisation, which is an integral part of the single market, affects the most vital sectors and transforms all social goods into commodities. the miller report welcomes the commission's proposal for the privatisation of public undertakings. it is raising the flag of competition and the four freedoms that fit into the embrace of capital. the opening up of the market for supply networks under the pretext of 'benefits' for consumers even extends to water supply. in the name of profit, the fact that water is a typical social good that no one can deprive of is completely ignored. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the only thing we need is the privatization of the air we breathe! we believe that workers should set their own priorities and fight against the decisions of capital, and we fight with them and the working class for new gains that meet the needs of the present.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, both the directive on the harmonisation of transparency requirements and the directive on financial services bring us today to the end of a difficult process, which has required enormous effort in the financial services sector, particularly on the part of the rapporteur, mrs villiers, on a subject with great technical and political difficulties, and without her tremendous work, i doubt whether we could have reached a conclusion. i would therefore like to congratulate her on all her efforts. the fact that we are reaching some definite conclusions today is an achievement of parliament and the economic committee and proves that legislative delays, when they occur, are not due to parliamentary austerity. the aim of the investment services directive, on which i would like to speak more as shadow rapporteur, is to reinvigorate the european passport for financial firms. the european passport is of course not a new institution, but it had been in existence for some time because of the difficulties created by the differences in the internal legislation of the member states in the cross-border activity of companies and, by extension, of course, in the free movement of capital in europe. the new directive proposes measures to ensure uniform and harmonised protection of investors, in particular small investors, in the various member states. it also proposes a level playing field between stock exchanges and investment companies. this is one of the most complex and thorny issues we have faced, because here we are faced with new phenomena in the market, so that entire stock exchanges are being turned into private companies and entire private companies are functioning as mini-exchanges in a way and within them they are settling a huge number of transactions. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission's proposal for a directive on the harmonization of the laws of the member states relating to the marketing of products for human consumption is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done in this area. the socialist group supports the agreement reached in the council on the investment services directive and, of course, supports the report on the harmonization of transparency requirements in financial services.
the commission. i would like to make a comment on mr titford first. this is not a tempus phase project, but a tempus project. my colleague, who is responsible for enlargement, asked me to clarify this. firstly, i would like to say the following about the question or the statements made by mrs stauner, too, that eur 1.8 billion was lost in 2002. that is a conclusion you simply cannot draw from the figures in the 280 report. the member states are obliged to report irregularities in the structural funds, the agricultural funds and the own resources. irregularities are defined as a breach of a regulation of a european nature. this may be an irregularity of a financial nature, but it may also be, for example, that an environmental regulation has been violated. so there is no financial loss at all. these reports must be made for the project as a whole. it is therefore by no means possible to conclude that this project as a whole is somehow equivalent to fraud. but if there is a financial irregularity, then there must be a recovery, and it must be made directly by the member states. so the amounts you mentioned are to be deducted from at least the recoveries. if you have a subsidy budget, of course, it would be a complete delusion to think that you could reduce fraud to zero. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. of course, it is annoying when reports are delayed or only partially reported. you are right, mr bösch, if there is a country with a report of zero, it is not exactly an indicator that everything is going well there, but it is an indicator that no checks are being carried out. even in those member states which may sometimes boast of good control, it is often found that this is not the case. look at the court of auditors report from 2000. unfortunately , it 's like this: in the country we come
