resulted in profound political, economic and social changes. in the report i have drawn up on behalf of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities, i present the situation of women in south-east europe as it has developed during the transitional period in relation to the previous regime. my report complements the reports on the union's relations with the countries of south-east europe and the progress of these countries under the stabilisation and association agreements. i intend to present a comprehensive picture of the position of women in a region with significant differences between countries, but also with great similarities in the problems they face. the report focuses mainly on the following areas: education and economic activity, participation in democratic institutions, violence against women and sexual exploitation, health and reproductive rights, trafficking in women and children. in most of the balkan countries, there has certainly been a considerable improvement in the situation of women over the last five years. however, the main problems remain and the issue of addressing them is not high on the political agenda of the countries in the region. in many areas change seems never to come, and where it does, it is very slow. the european union has also been involved in the process of developing a new policy on women's rights, which is based on the principle of equal treatment for men and women. it is characteristic that women from various minority groups, such as the roma, receive little or no state care. the report analyses the problems, giving specific examples of the weaknesses in each country nominally, in order to send a strong message to the governments of these countries to develop immediate actions and initiatives to combat discrimination against women and actively promote gender equality. in the field of education and the economy, the profound changes have also greatly diversified the positions and capacities of different generations of women. demographic trends today show that family life is no longer a primary life goal for women, who are more interested in their financial independence and professional development, thus investing more time and energy in education. in many countries in the region, such as greece, the majority of university students are women. in terms of working life, women are more often employees, i.e. employed in the tertiary sector, in services, and less often employers than men, and they are also less likely to be selfemployed. in sectors where women are predominantly employed, wages are usually low and with little prospect of development. i also note the high rates of unemployment and poverty among women in the balkans. the situation regarding women's participation in political decision-making is rather disappointing for most countries, but there is a continuing trend towards an increase in this participation. one of the main similarities between countries in the field of policy is the rapid expansion of non-governmental organisations active in women's rights and making a significant contribution to strengthening women's position in employment and politics. it should be noted, however, that compared to other regions of europe such as central europe, the balkan countries have the highest level of exclusion of women from political decision-making positions. on women's health and reproductive rights, i would stress that particularly stressful living conditions, the survival economy based on the intensive exploitation of women's human resources, the increase in violence against women and the fact that in many countries health care systems have collapsed, put women's health at real risk. in the balkan countries too, domestic violence is often a problem of dramatic proportions, but mainly a problem of inadequate recognition and response. this problem, along with sex trafficking, has taken on explosive proportions. however, across the region there is a lack of serious and systematic statistics on the problem, a lack of adequate legislation, monitoring and guidance, and a lack of financial and psychological support for victims. as regards the trafficking of women, south-eastern europe in general has been identified in recent years as a region where trafficking networks operate almost unhindered. the sex trade thrives because it's economically viable, it's a key part of the desire economy and part of the consumer society. the trafficking in persons in the region is in fact an industry, made up of small and large-scale enterprises, local and international networks. the industry has political support and financial resources in the countries of origin, transit and destination in the form of corrupt officials, and i would like to cite as a typical example the sex slavery scandal in montenegro, where a moldovan woman, a victim of human trafficking, identified well-known politicians as clients or involved in the trafficking of white flesh. i should also point out that the peacekeeping forces in the region have increased this phenomenon. in conclusion, i would like to say that the path to economic and social development in south-east europe and respect for women's rights is certainly through their integration into european structures, and i would like to recall the thessaloniki european council in june 2003, which stressed its support for the european perspective of the western balkan countries. the european union must be able to take action to ensure that the rights of the child are respected and that the rights of the child are protected. the role of women in the reconciliation, peaceful coexistence and development of the countries of south-east europe should be assessed by the european union and strengthened in every way.
commission. mr president, the commission welcomes the adoption of the karamanou report on women in south-east europe and the many valuable observations and proposals which it contains. in its relations with the countries of south-east europe the eu is striving to promote stability, democracy, the rule of law and respect for human and minority rights, including of course gender equality. the commission pays the utmost attention to ensuring that gender issues are integrated into the mainstream of all european community assistance programmes as a cross-cutting issue. it is nevertheless clear that in a number of countries the situation is far from ideal and is a problem of great political importance. the thessaloniki agenda for the western balkans as endorsed by the eu-western balkans summit in june 2003 specifically states that support for activities to defend women's rights and improve their situation should be better reflected in eu policies towards the region. involvement of non-governmental organisations, civil society and local authorities in eu-supported policies and activities is of paramount importance. regarding trafficking in human beings, in the thessaloniki agenda the eu also urges the countries of the region to act both domestically and regionally in line with the principles of the eu and proposals contained in the brussels declaration of september 2002. the eu assigns high priority to victim assistance, training programmes for competent bodies, exchange of information, public awareness, and strategy development. in this context, coordination of all international actors is essential. most recently the commission has pointed out in its 2004 report on the stablisation and association process for south-east europe that gender is insufficiently ensured and domestic violence remains a problem in several countries. although improvements have been made in certain countries on issues such as equal salaries, equal treatment of men and women concerning employment, training, career advancement and working conditions, and women's participation in government, progress is generally slow and insufficient. under the regional cards programme for 2002, the commission published an open call for submission of proposals for support for the rule of law, good governance, public accountability and freedom of opinion. promoting the equal participation of men and women in the democratisation process was one of the priorities of the card stabilisation programme for 2002. of the twenty-two projects selected for funding which are ongoing until 2005, one is funding a women's leadership programme and another a project on women in media. the rest relate to work with various representatives of civil society to improve the access of citizens more generally to the democratic process. with regard to the 2003 cards regional programme on democratic stabilisation, an open call for submissions was issued recently and the deadline for receiving proposals is 28 april. while this programme does not specifically target women, it supports equality of men and women and the participation of diverse civil society groups and associations at local grassroots level in the process of strengthening democratic structures. furthermore, an additional call for proposals under the 2003 cards regional programme is due to be issued soon. the programme proposes to strengthen capacities of civil society network associations in order to deliver better services to their member organisations. this constitutes a logical continuation of the 2002 initiatives and we expect and hope that women's associations will submit sound proposals in response to this call. finally, a new cards multiannual programming cycle is currently under preparation and the suggestions in the karamanou report will be duly taken into account in the context of this exercise. as pointed out in the report, problems have also occurred and still exist in certain candidate countries. in order to address this situation, from 1999 to 2003 gender equality projects under the phare national programme and meda totalled more than eur 13 million. during the same period, participation by the candidate countries in the community programmes involved close to eur 1 million and gender equality projects financed under civil society programmes totalled eur 2.5 million. once they are members of the european union, these states will obviously be subject to the same obligations and benefit from the same instruments as other member states. issues related to gender equality and the improvement of women's employment are raised in the regular reports on bulgaria, romania and turkey. this matter is being dealt with in the negotiations on the union's social acquis for the first two countries. furthermore, as pointed out by the honourable member, more that 20 projects have been financed for civil society actions to improve women's conditions. recent legislation adopted in the three countries is improving the legal situation of women and the commission is following the implementation of this legislation very closely.
the balkans are a region of vital importance to the european union. my political group has supported their european orientation and all the development and cooperation procedures that have been put in place. historical experience and everyday life in the european union demonstrate that development and prosperity cannot take place without democracy, without respect for human rights and special attention to women's rights, without the exploitation of human resources of both sexes, without a vibrant civil society. i would therefore like to welcome the initiative taken by mrs karamanou to draw up a report on women's rights in the balkans. in the context of the monitoring of the stabilisation and association agreements, the committee on women's rights has drawn up a number of opinions - i had the honour of being the author - in which it stresses the problems facing women in the balkans and the need to integrate equality into all policies. the seriousness of the issues, as the rapporteur has pointed out in her report, and the challenge that the tackling of these issues poses to the development of the region and to good relations of cooperation with the european union, deserve a special report. i welcome the opportunity and the statement by commissioner nielson to include in the new cards programme many of the proposals contained in her report. in conclusion, i would like to say that we are both responsible for this challenge. we as the european union must put all the mechanisms and possibilities in place for a good cooperation approach with the balkans, but the balkan countries must also take seriously the fact that democracy and quality are the best guarantees for their development and for their integration into the european union.
mr president, commissioner, there is often confirmation in the political sphere that small policies, when implemented, produce big results and large policies, when announced but not implemented, produce nothing. in this particular case, the ottawa convention, which was signed seven years ago and has a very short period of application, has proved its applicability and effectiveness, so it is a highly successful policy. the choice of nairobi as the first conference to review this convention is particularly symbolic, because we all know that sub-saharan africa is one of the regions with the highest concentration of antipersonnel mines, since mines are considered the weapon of the poor. i take this opportunity to note that my country, which several years ago remained outside this convention, has recently, under the leadership of then foreign minister george papandreou, agreed to the convention and ratified and implemented it in relation to its border with turkey, where for reasons we all understand there was a cold front of opposition. the ottawa convention has the ultimate objective of overcoming the landmine problem through coordinated action against landmines on an international scale within the next 10 to 15 years. this remains a challenge, as over 60 countries report a problem with this issue, and 20 of them are very seriously affected. the european experience itself since the end of the cold war, and the recent experience in the balkans, shows that this is a major problem on our continent. of course, in asia, africa and latin america, where hundreds of millions of mines are laid every day, undermining health and economic development, the problem is even greater. the united nations plays the central role in organising and coordinating international efforts, with the european union providing the largest contribution. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the european parliament has taken many initiatives since 1992 to ban this deadly weapon, which destroys lives and prevents economic development. but our greatest contribution has been to the implementation of these programmes in many, many parts of the world. in afghanistan, iraq, the balkans, in dozens of countries where non-governmental and governmental organizations are engaged in the removal of antipersonnel mines. but beyond the clearing of mines, they must do much more for education, for research, for general awareness of the danger and its prevention. the nairobi meeting provides a great opportunity to implement all the aspects of the ottawa convention which have not been implemented in any way because of some technical deficiencies, to complete the convention, to complete it and to give the world a new convention, albeit of small size, but of great effectiveness for peace and security in the developing and especially poor areas of the world.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, today is a good time to look at our economic policy from a broader perspective, to look at the past of europe of 15 and at the future of europe of 25. the first key point to note is that in the european union we do not have a legal basis for a common, truly common economic policy because the union has no competence to formulate, it has only the competence to coordinate the economic policies of the member states. this fragmentation of economic policy is not something that can be attributed to the conditions of globalization that we are living in today. the second point is that we need a major strategic initiative capable of mobilizing the forces of the european economy. the third point is that, in addition to the grand strategic initiative, the european economy needs a grand strategic objective. the accession of our ten new member states offers the opportunity for a major initiative which can inspire our efforts. the economies of eastern europe can become the driving force of the new european economy, if we, the old europe, can find the courage and the inspiration to emulate the united states of america and mark the end of the cold war with a kind of marshall plan for eastern europe, as the united states did at the end of the second world war. as for the great objective, it was given to us by the draft of the new european constitution which, for the first time in the history of the treaties, placed full employment in the basic article 3 of the union's objectives, while rejecting the european central bank's demand for equal treatment of the objective of price stability. the last point is important and must be respected, but it is not more important, it is never more important than the objective of full european model. the european union must be able to take action to combat poverty and social exclusion, and to ensure that the social dimension of the internal market is fully reflected in the social dimension.
mr president, i would first like to welcome today's first decision by the enlarged european parliament, which rejected the council's attempt to bring back the issue of concluding an agreement on the processing and transfer of european passengers' personal data by airlines to the us department of homeland security. the question of freedom and security in the europe of 25 which we are discussing today is directly linked, commissioner, to the security of the olympic games which are being held this summer in greece. the safety of the games has been a constant headache since ancient times, and the truce was the most serious part of the preparation for the athletes and spectators to arrive safely at olympia. i believe, as romantic as it sounds, that promoting the idea of a ceasefire in the modern age and educating people in peaceful coexistence would cost much less and have more lasting effects than strengthening repressive forces and using military means. for the europe of 25, the 2004 olympics are a golden opportunity to promote the idea of the armistice, that is, the ideals of peace, cooperation, dialogue and the rejection of violence as a means of resolving disputes, on a global scale. unfortunately, the reality of the modern world speaks of an intensity of blind violence, hatred and terrorism. the publication of photographs of torture and humiliation of prisoners in iraq shows the extent of the brutality and barbarity that people can be subjected to when they are involved in wars and acts of violence. but it also shows the degree of freedom of the press that we have achieved and must preserve. the commissioner, mr president, has already said that the european union is not prepared to accept the idea of a european union which is not based on the united nations, but on the united nations. will political leaders realise that a different strategy is needed to counter terrorism and strengthen security? mr president, if lisbon has taught us anything, it is that strengthening the competitiveness of the european economy, of business, is not enough to create jobs, social cohesion and protect the environment. the commission has also been asked to present a report on the implementation of the lisbon strategy, which is a very important step in the process of european integration. the dutch presidency's programme seems to ignore them. it proposes a series of measures to reduce administrative controls on the market, reduce costs, even hinting at an increase in working hours, but makes no mention of social sustainability. the dutch presidency is in a sense suggesting that the liberalisation of the market will automatically lead to employment. but this is precisely the philosophy that lisbon rejects, and that is why it puts emphasis on the rest. we therefore want to hear from the dutch presidency specific policies for full employment and sustainable growth: simple things. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the common organization of the market in wine and spirits, which is to be adopted by the council on 1 january, is a very good one. but we can't have it both ways! for us, 1.24% of gdp is the minimum threshold for financing the new european union. as regards immigration - if i understand the dutch presidency correctly - it can be treated primarily as a police matter and immigrants as potential criminals! commission madam president, i share the concern of the honourable member. indeed, doping is killing the sport. it must be remembered, however, that in the field of anti-doping we only exercise community competence in a subsidiary way: the commission can therefore only act to supplement and strengthen the action taken by sports organisations and the member states. but we did it anyway. in 1999, the commission adopted a community support plan for the fight against doping and indicated possible ways of combating it. i must say that the european parliament has fully supported the commission, while there has been little or no reaction from the council, despite which we have launched concrete actions in support of the anti-doping fight, analytical actions, for example on the situation of young people in the gymnasiums - catastrophic - on the socio-economic factors of doping, on the actions to be taken to protect young people. at the same time, we have helped the member states to build, together with the sports world and the federations, the world anti-doping agency. our practical contribution to this world anti-doping agency has been the training of anti-doping controllers to monitor athletes. in the field of health programmes, too, actions have been undertaken to stimulate the efforts of the member states in the field of prevention of drug use and abuse among young people. of course, the area of prevention is also extremely important and in this context i would like to inform you that 2004 has been declared the 'european year of education through sport'. throughout the year, thousands of projects were set up with schools, sports federations and local and regional authorities to raise awareness among young people of the dangers of doping and to encourage them to respect the principle of fair play in sport. of course, as long as there is no clear and precise legal basis, we will be unable to do more. however, we have worked on the development of a legal basis. since my appointment as commissioner responsible for sport in 1999, i have regularly convened an informal council of ministers for sport - there could be no formal council - to bring them together to work towards this. i have also regularly met with european sports federations to encourage them to work together to combat the scourge of doping. finally, the convention has crowned all this work by giving us its support and by proposing an article in the constitutional treaty which provides that the union's action must aim in particular at protecting the physical and moral integrity of young people, especially young sportsmen. once the treaty is adopted, we will have a real legal basis and formal meetings of sports ministers can take place and concrete action can be taken in this direction. we are all looking forward to the new treaty.
the situation with the measures mentioned has not only not improved but has worsened. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the use of these substances, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. when a sports team is a business, it and its sponsors aim to increase profits and, therefore, the athlete's victory at all costs is an aim to maximize profits. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's sporting heritage is protected. i would therefore like to ask you whether you intend to take measures to ensure that sport is a genuine social good and that it is not driven by the logic of profit and sponsorship. are you willing to cut the umbilical cord between sports and business? that's the big question.
commission madam president, i will try to extract a question from a statement, and i will say that i agree with the honourable member that doping is a scourge. i do not agree with two of his statements. first, it argues that there is a clear difference between amateur and professional sport. unfortunately, our studies have revealed that amateur sports are also affected by this phenomenon. so we must not only fight doping in professional sports but also in amateur sports and we must be concerned about public health, since doping is becoming a real public health problem. the second statement by the honourable member which i dispute is the idea that, despite all the measures implemented, the situation has not only not improved, but has on the contrary worsened. well, the answer is very simple: as long as we deny the existence of doping and the need for controls, offenders will not be worried. now that everyone admits that doping is everywhere and we have set up controls with the international olympic committee and the world anti-doping agency, we obviously catch those who dop and that's a good thing, because the more we catch, the fewer will take the risk of doping, and the more we send a positive signal that "no, doping will not pass" and that a really, really fierce fight is being waged against this scourge that is killing the sport.
commission madam president, as i have just explained, the european year of education through sport was created because a european year of sport could not be created without a legal basis to finance it. i therefore had to move the problem to education as a legal basis, so that i could take concrete action in the field of sport and the protection of young athletes. so what can we do when the treaty comes into force? well, we can take direct action on sports. firstly, there will be a council of ministers for sport which will be empowered to make proposals in conjunction with the commission. the commission itself will be able to propose action programmes, financed of course by the european union, to help sports federations, governments, educators, clubs to move forward. so this is gonna change everything. i believe that everything that has been done so far has been in preparation for this new treaty, but we will need a genuine legal basis to be able to make a policy that is more solid, more visible. we have done as much as we can and more than we can do, given the possibilities.

vice-president of the commission. madam president, as the honourable member knows, all industries and all the elements in the chain have an obligation to label and indicate the presence of genetically modified organisms in the composition. fraud may occur, but it will be up to the various authorities in the european union to ensure that it does not. the important thing is that the authorization of a genetically modified organism should be accompanied by the possibility of its traceability, that is, that there are systems which allow its detection, if it is incorporated into any product. we all know that there is fraud in the use of hormones, for example. as a former minister of agriculture, i can tell you that we must be very vigilant and vigilant to prevent such fraud and to prosecute it if it occurs. the important thing is that the consumer can make a choice. as regards the second point, you speak of unfair competition with regard to farmers. unfortunately, this is not unfair competition. if competition is unfair, it will be in respect of the traditional varieties of the various products. the unfair competition that nk 302 may have to face is competition with the traditional maize varieties that have been grown in europe and throughout the world. farmers may or may not use it. they have the opportunity to use any of the varieties. and the competition, if there is any unfair competition, will be between the lab varieties and the natural ones. i believe that there is also a responsibility on the part of citizens and politicians. we have in our hands the choice of what we want: traditional varieties or new laboratory varieties. it is the free choice of consumers themselves that will ultimately decide how this issue develops.

commission madam president, the question concerns the conclusions drawn by the commission from the abolition of quantitative restrictions on international trade in textiles, which will take effect on 31 december this year, in accordance with a decision taken at multilateral level within the world trade organisation ten years ago. we all agreed together, ten years ago, that these quantitative restrictions would be lifted by the end of this year. now the deadline is coming, and it raises - understandably, even though we have had all the time to prepare for it for ten years with gradual liberalization - a certain anxiety, as well as fears and difficulties to which the commission has endeavoured to respond. the general strategy we have adopted is to regard the european textile and clothing industry, as the author himself does, not as an industry of the past - whose gradual disappearance is to be managed more or less cleanly from a social and political point of view - but on the contrary as a sector of the future. the textile and clothing industry in europe is a competitive, modern, innovative and productive industry. europe is the continent where the textile and clothing sector is the most modern and productive. however, the completion of the elimination of quantitative restrictions poses a challenge to our competitiveness which we must be able to meet. and that is what the commission did when it proposed lines of action last june. we worked with a high-level group representing the commission, the member states, various players in the sector, producers, distributors and trade union representatives; i have personally participated in the work of this group, which has produced a number of conclusions. here's the gist of it. what we propose to do is to start by monitoring international trade a little more closely, and in particular our imports. we will therefore set up a near real-time monitoring system by the end of this year so that the figures, and in particular any sudden increases in imports, can be more easily identified and that we can draw the necessary conclusions in accordance with our wto rights. we will also pay greater attention to international competition issues, and in particular to illegal practices such as dumping, fraud, protection of intellectual property, which are all, of course, cancers that need to be monitored very closely. the second element is the conquest of foreign markets. in our view, this remains the most effective weapon for securing the future of the sector, whether it is large markets which remain protected, such as the american market in a number of cases, or whether it is potentially important markets for us, namely a number of markets in developing countries - not all of them - which, in the textile and clothing sector, are now potential importers of european products, particularly those with high added value. the whole discussion, which will be mainly on tariffs and non-tariff barriers, will be held in the context of the doha round of trade negotiations. we are therefore at the heart of this international negotiation. i would add a regional dimension to this multilateral work, which concerns the euro-mediterranean area, on which we have been working with our partners around the mediterranean, namely the countries of the europe of the 25, of course, and the candidate countries or those which are likely to become such as turkey, the idea being to establish a kind of pan-european integrated textile and clothing area with, for example, the help of a pan-euro-mediterranean cumul of origin which we have negotiated, and up to this year included. finally, a very brief word on china, which is obviously the most formidable competitor in this matter, given the efficiency, productivity and wage levels in that country. we have set up a system of bilateral dialogue with the chinese on textile trade to examine ways of avoiding too great a disruption of trade as a result of the elimination of quotas. in addition to this government-to-government and administration-to-administration dialogue, we have established a dialogue between european and chinese business circles, the first meeting of which took place very recently. all these measures, which were taken up in the commission's initial communication last october and which were discussed in the context of this work of the high-level group, will be the subject of a new commission communication, which is currently scheduled for october. so we're going to approach this year-end deadline with a lot of awareness, but also with a toolbox that has been seriously improved in recent times.
madam president, although i am wasting valuable time, i would also like to publicly congratulate the commissioner, mr lamie, on the work he has done from the portfolio he has managed so far as commissioner. my supplementary question is what general measures of harmonization, reciprocity and abolition of non-trade barriers the commission intends to take, both at multilateral and bilateral level, and in the context of interregional cooperation. the commission has already made a number of proposals, and i am sure that the commission will be able to take them up in the next few months.
the commission. i would like to thank mr papadimoulis because his question allows me to clarify certain issues relating to contractors in greece - one of the issues that has arisen and, of course, to clarify matters concerning the directive on fixed-term work. the directive requires member states to take measures to improve the quality of fixedterm work by ensuring the application of the principle of non-discrimination and to prevent abuse caused by the use of successive fixedterm contracts. the way in which this is achieved depends to a large extent on the member states themselves. they may, for example, set out objective reasons justifying the renewal of such contracts, the maximum total duration of successive contracts and the maximum number of renewals of such contracts. different solutions may be available for different branches of employment or for different categories of workers, provided that the rules in place are sufficient to prevent abuse. it should be noted that this directive does not require a member state to take measures to convert fixedterm contracts into permanent contracts. many countries have such rules as part of a package of measures to prevent abuse, but the directive itself does not establish a right for fixedterm workers to become permanent workers. as regards the new greek presidential decree, article 10 on transitional provisions provides for the conversion of certain successive contracts into contracts of indefinite duration. however, as this conversion is not a requirement of the community directive, the manner and criteria by which it is achieved and the number and categories of contractors it covers cannot be subject to control by the european commission. the first legislative act to which mr papadimoulis refers to the implementation of the directive in greece was indeed not satisfactory as regards the measures to prevent abuse by successive fixed-term contracts. the european commission's services were in contact with the greek authorities at the time in order to find solutions more quickly and to avoid resorting to legal proceedings. greece, as i mentioned earlier, has since adopted a new presidential decree, which changes the data of the previous presidential decree regarding the public sector. apart from the conversion of some fixed-term contracts into permanent positions, the general rule of the new presidential decree is that fixed-term contracts exceeding the maximum duration of 24 months as defined in the presidential decree are automatically void and may justify the payment of compensation to workers and the imposition of sanctions, including criminal, on employers. the commission's preliminary examination has shown that the rules on successive fixedterm contracts in the new decree are not contrary to the directive on fixedterm work. however, since the decree only covers the public sector, the situation must also be addressed for the private sector. as soon as greece notifies the commission of any additional relevant legislation implementing the directive, we will assess whether it complies with community law and whether it adopts the necessary measures where appropriate.
commissioner, if we assume that greece, our common home, would implement the community directive we are talking about in time, even after the extension it received in 2002, thousands of people, with the provisions of the presidential decree to which you referred, would have secured the conversion of their contract into an indefinite contract. my question is this: why should the delay of the greek authorities for two years be paid for by thousands of workers exposed to abuses by employers, whether public or private?

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, despite the fact that a number of states are now openly violating the criteria of the stability pact by serving the narrow interests of the monopolies, the european union insists on its maintenance and even stresses the need for greater strictness in its application in order to intensify the pressure for the adoption of new anti-people measures. the proposed amendments, combined with the 3% budget deficit and 60% public debt criteria, make the stability pact even more reactionary in order to speed up the implementation of the anti-people lisbon strategy. a coordinated increase in taxation of employees is being promoted to create budget surpluses. they put greater control on national budgets to strengthen the competitiveness of european monopoly firms by reducing taxation and providing new privileges for capital. they put a heavy weight on public debt in order to push for immediate changes in pension systems, further commercialization of health care, reduction of social spending and faster promotion of capitalist restructuring to make labor cheaper and increase the profitability of big business. the proposed revision confirms that the only way to deal with the stability pact, and the european union's anti-people policy in general, is through indiscipline, workers' disobedience and the escalation of the workers' struggle.
the commission's proposals for changes to the stability pact, mr president, do not affect its neoliberal philosophy. they serve the particular needs - to tell the truth - of the german and french governments and will burden countries like greece with a particularly high public debt, a debt that will grow because of the very expensive olympic games and high defence spending. these changes to the pact do not address the critical problems: low growth rates, high unemployment, large social and regional disparities, problems which have plagued the course of the european union in recent years. the european united left, contrary to the criticism of european bankers who express the utmost neoliberal orthodoxy, calls for a radical reform of the stability pact. we are calling for a european pact for employment, growth and social cohesion which will introduce social criteria with measurable and verifiable indicators and provide for penalties and sanctions even for countries which violate them, for example, countries which maintain high unemployment.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would agree that the stability pact has been useful. but it needs to be changed because it has not always been applied correctly or fairly. in some countries the commission has exhausted its rigor and in others the council has exhausted its indulgence. i agree that we must take account not only of the real deficit but also of the structural deficit, that is to say, we must ignore short-term cyclical fluctuations in gdp. if we had done that, germany would not have had a problem in 2002, in 2003, and not this year. france, on the other hand, would have had a problem in 2002 and 2003, but not this year. a source of friction would have been eliminated. i also agree that we should not neglect the debt. but that means we have to use the primary structural deficit, that is, subtracting the cost of debt relief. i also agree that it should be examined whether a country has had excessive expenditure for which it is not solely responsible, as happened with greece and the extraordinary burdens of the olympic games. finally, mr president, i want an agreement that will achieve the stability of the euro area and not the unequal treatment of its members, an agreement with a long-term strategy and not with spasmodic reactions, ensuring the lisbon objectives without plunging the economies into a vicious cycle of recession. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

commission mr president, this is not my last appearance before you, since i shall have the opportunity to see you again in plenary in october, but it would have been the last i would have seen in this coincidence, which is why pierre moscovici is chairing this sitting, a sign, which is sometimes sent to me from elsewhere. unlike my usual practice, i will not be able to give a detailed answer to each and every one of you, since i have only a few minutes. i shall confine myself to the essential, in the usual telegraphic style. mrs martens asks me whether the "everything but arms" regime is going to be changed. no, it's not going to be changed and there's no graduation in the "everything but guns" regime. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the gsp to include the mediterranean countries, the countries of the former soviet union and the countries of central and eastern europe. she's right, you know. let us simply know that among the reasons why a number of countries do not have access to our market, although they do have access by law, there is everything to do with sanitary and phytosanitary standards, problems of standards, quality and certification, which are not the responsibility of tariffs. there will therefore always be a difference between theoretical access and practical access, which is due to non-tariff barriers, for which, unfortunately, we cannot establish a specific regime for developing countries: we are not going to establish a tolerance regime for antibiotics in shrimp for everyone and then a specific, favourable regime for shrimp in developing countries. unfortunately, we cannot do it that way! mrs castex suggests that i should involve the social partners in cases where the benefit of these preferences is linked to compliance with social clauses: we are already doing that, mrs castex, and we will continue to do so. for example, the inquiry we are currently conducting into the respect for trade union rights in belarus, which could lead to the union withdrawing the benefit of these preferences from that country, was triggered by a complaint from the european trade union confederation. that is also my answer to mr markov on this point. i'm being asked if the number of beneficiaries is going to be revised. of course, the number of beneficiaries will be revised, based on a criterion, namely market share. i have already said to you, and i say again, that the number of beneficiaries will be affected very little by this system. i should also point out that we are not talking about a country or a product when we talk about graduation, but about a country/product pair - country 'x', product 'y' - which is graduated. caroline lucas is asking me again about the timetable: i believe i explained to you our constraints, particularly those arising from the indian litigation. as for the rules of origin, which she and others - mrs van lancker and mrs glenys kinnock - have insisted on, just know this: suppose we withdraw the benefit of the scheme from china, which is a huge exporter of textile clothing, and allow sri lanka, which exports clothing, to source fabric from china, which, if i understand correctly, mrs lucas would like us to do. in such a case, we are giving back to china, through the back door, if i may say, in the textile sector, the advantage we are taking away from it through the front door. so we can't put ourselves in that kind of contradiction. on the other hand, if sri lanka wants to source cloth from the region around it, we are probably - i have to check this with my colleagues - open to that. i'm almost done with it. mr. louis doesn't like the wto, so he can go ahead. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council by the end of the year. it's obviously a caricature. i have not heard that from any other member during this debate. i can only see one point in his speech where he agrees with the commission, which is not bad enough, and that is that the gsp must be preserved - i would even go a little further, improved. mr. sturdy agrees with the tech support connection. mrs kinnock and mrs van lancker want details on the thresholds and the list, either conventions or sanctions. these details will be available on 20 october when the commission takes a decision on my proposal. mrs kinnock wants to see more clarity in the relationship between economic partnership agreements and these unilateral regimes. these are two different systems: economic partnership agreements are intended to promote regional integration within the acp and the gsp, for the time being, except through the rules of origin, does not have this objective. i have finished with that, mr president. i am glad that once again in trade policy parliament has the opportunity to debate a week before the commission itself, which shows that we have made some progress in terms of transparency.
commission mr president, ladies and gentlemen, today, at one of your first plenary sessions, you wanted to put the fight against doping on the agenda. this wish clearly reflects the priority given by parliament to the fight against this scourge which damages the image of sport and the health of sportsmen. at the moment, doping is not only a problem of fair competition, it is also a public health problem. the commission shares your determination in this respect. as you know, for the last five years i have been making every effort to get doping on the community agenda and to take as effective action as possible within the limits of our powers. today, as i leave this portfolio to which i have devoted a great deal of energy, i thank you for giving me the opportunity to take stock of our common progress and to outline the paths for the future. the balance sheet is as follows: i believe that in five years we have done the maximum possible within the existing legal framework, also taking into account the reluctance of several member states and of part of the sports movement to consider the community level as a central level in the fight against doping. you know, sports is not yet a community competence. the commission could only intervene in strict compliance with its powers. the commission can only intervene in this area in order to supplement and strengthen the action taken by sports organisations and the member states. in recent years we have had to use pedagogy in this area to explain what our powers are and what our limits are. on the one hand we were asked why the commission was not taking more initiatives, and on the other hand we were told that we were taking initiatives to harmonize the procedures for monitoring the lists of doping substances, in other words that we were taking over powers which we did not have. in fact, we have done a lot in our field of competence: information, prevention, training, awareness-raising, project support - for example, the athlete's passport, multimedia information tools, scientific studies. we have encouraged dialogue, we have encouraged cooperation. what does that mean, in practical terms? the beginning of my mandate coincided with a period when doping was in the news, with the events of the 1998 tour de france. we have seen that national responses are no longer sufficient and that cooperation, or even coordination, between member states is essential. at the end of 1999, i asked the european ethics group, then chaired by mrs lenoir, to give us its opinion. it was the first time that this body had spoken on the subject of doping and this opinion has been extremely useful in the years since and will remain so in the future. he inspired everything we do. in december 1999, a community support plan for the fight against doping was adopted by the commission. this support plan was welcomed by parliament. at that time mrs zabel was the rapporteur. we asked for the concern about doping to be taken into account in all community policies. under this plan, and with the help of parliament, an annual budget of €5 million has been allocated to anti-doping projects - studies, research, conferences, actions. we have thus been able to support projects which have advanced both information and research in this field. i would like to mention, for example, our study on doping in fitness centres, projects involving young people and a compilation of laws and regulations in the member states. three studies carried out by external consultants, which focused on the socioeconomic factors of doping, youth protection and the european communication strategy. now let's talk about the wada, the world anti-doping agency. i have personally invested a great deal in the creation of the agency, and i have fought above all to uphold principles in the interests of efficiency, so that the new agency can live up to the wishes of those who want zero tolerance to be applied to doping. i had insisted on the need for this agency to be independent and transparent. the genesis of wada was in accordance with our wishes; and the agency was set up in 1999. however, because of incompatibility between the wada budgetary rules and procedures and community law, no community funding could be granted and i do not see any change in this in the future. the draft constitutional treaty, which contains a reference to sport, will not bring any change on this point. it should be noted that wada is doing an honourable job. i want it to become an international public law institution, which i think will make it more effective and credible than the present body, which is governed by private law, which is a problem. finally, the fight against doping is by definition an international phenomenon. this is one of the reasons why we held the marrakesh conference in january 2002, which gave us the opportunity to reflect on the problem of doping with our colleagues in the mediterranean. i would also like to mention the european year of education through sport, during which we have set up a system of information and warning for our young people, and thus a whole system of prevention. prevention is the key word, because we are not here to be repressive, and that is why the commission must focus on the preventive aspect. it must tackle the factors that contribute to creating an environment favourable to doping: the status and training of sports trainers, the role of the medical community, the pressure for success from sponsors. so you see, in five years, a very important work has been done, a joint work, in which i have always been able to count on the full support of parliament. in future, this responsibility will be borne by the new commissioner figel, who, i know, is aware of the importance of this issue. let me make two points, mr president. on the one hand, the constitutional treaty is a source of hope in this area because it says that the physical and moral integrity of young people, especially young sportsmen, must be protected. we will thus be able to strengthen support for the member states and the federations, which will always retain primary responsibility, but we will also be able to support the action of the states in the field of prevention, information and awareness-raising. i would also mention a very important element for all the action that will follow, namely the meca-medina court's judgment of 30 september, which has the merit of clarifying the place of the fight against doping in the community legal environment and which has come to confirm the commission's position as i have been defending it for five years, which i welcome. he said that the fight against doping had no economic purpose. the court therefore held that the ioc's anti-doping rules did not fall within the scope of community competition law. it aims to preserve the sportsmanship and health of athletes. in other words, it is a sporting rule, which leaves the predominance of sporting federations to regulate the matter. mr president, i am very pleased with this conclusion and the wording of this judgment. for the last five years i have been trying to show that the commission is not only interested in sport as an economic phenomenon, but that sport is above all a social activity with an educational and ethical content. and i know that this is the path my successor will follow, because it is important that this parliament, as it has always supported me, will support mr figel in this matter. we have a responsibility to our society. as sport is a key element of this society, we have a responsibility in the field of sport.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mrs reding, we have recently seen that fair play, which is the basic principle of sport and of the olympic games, has become a competitive sport. the noble amylase and the athletic ideal have now been tarnished by the athlete's intense quest to win and excel. in the context of this enormous effort and great competition, science is breaking in with new solutions promising great results. but today i would like to focus on the cases of women and youth. the use of anabolic substances by female athletes, especially young women, damages their femininity, and abuse poses serious health risks, often leading to the loss of motherhood. so in a broader context, the devastating effects of anabolic drugs on younger generations who are trying to emulate their idols are becoming more acute and the need for society to protect us is seen as imperative. we are therefore faced with a new reality, the well-known doping. these products are widely marketed in many forms, either on the internet, on television and in the mail, and even more so in gyms and sports venues, as the media have reported. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done. i would remind you that the turnover from the trafficking of anabolic substances is over two billion euros a year in europe and about six billion euros a year worldwide. the wada may be adding to the list of banned anabolic substances almost daily, but the social problem is exacerbated by the young people who are victims of the breakdown of their standards. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. the european union's intervention in this major social problem which is damaging the image of sport and public health in writing. the proposed amendment to regulation 1268/1999 aims to approve preaccession measures concerning agriculture and rural development in the candidate countries of central and eastern europe which did not join in may 2004, romania and bulgaria. the aim of these measures is to adapt the agricultural economies of the applicant countries to the cap so that they can be integrated in the near future. the kke group in the european parliament is opposed to eu enlargement because it would be to the detriment of the peoples of these countries, as it is to the detriment of the living standards and rights of the greek people. but it also disagrees with the cap because its main objective is to reduce the rural economies of the applicant countries by imposing low quotas and by eliminating small and medium-sized farmers so that the land is concentrated in a few large farmers. the commission also considers that the community's agricultural policy should be geared to the needs of the less-favoured regions and that the community should be able to take account of the specific needs of the less-favoured regions.

mr president, the high oil prices and the current dependence of the world economy on it, in addition to the obvious concern in every economy, have begun to cause economic suffocation in households and businesses. the present price increase appears not merely to be temporary or speculative but also to reflect structural changes, capable of leading to multiple revisions of the world economy. the development of the asian zone and a number of geopolitical factors are among the factors that have created the current economic disruption from oil. it is therefore not at all improbable that oil prices will stabilize at levels well above what we have considered normal in the past. the commission itself has stated that a 25% increase in the price of oil this year will reduce the rate of growth by 0.17% for the european union. these negative developments make it imperative that the commission and the council take immediate action and also take action in the medium term. in a period of economic recession, of pressure to increase inflationary trends, of a review of the fiscal policy of the stability and growth pact and of the effectiveness of the support mechanisms of the lisbon agreement, we cannot, commissioner, confine ourselves to statements and reports that there is no problem in substance. the european parliament would like to reconsider ways - the commission contributes to the development of the northern regions of greece through the structural funds, not only through the implementation of operational programmes in individual sectors, but also through the interreg programmes implemented under the community initiatives. these programmes provide for the financing of crossborder, transnational and transregional cooperation activities in the regions of the eu member states. under the third community support framework for greece, covering the years 2000 to 2006, the european union is co-financing structural measures in the greek regions. in this context, programmes have been agreed for the regions of western macedonia, central macedonia and eastern macedonia and thrace. the aim of this measure is to promote development and structural adjustment. the total public expenditure planned for these measures in the three regions concerned is eur 2.7 billion, of which the structural funds will contribute eur 2 billion, or 75%. while the measures related to improving local infrastructure are progressing very well, the measures to improve the dynamism of the local economy and the labour market and to exploit the potential of human resources have unfortunately been delayed. this is because of the difficulty of implementing such measures. the competent authorities are often overwhelmed by the need to implement these very complex and very demanding programmes. in addition to these funds, the regions mentioned also receive substantial funds from the operational programmes of the community support framework for greece, which are implemented throughout the country. over 65% of the structural funds allocated to greece are managed through these horizontal programmes. the northern regions are also involved in the following crossborder programmes: firstly, the interreg programme for greece and turkey, which has a budget of eur 46.6 million. this programme was approved at the end of 2003; no measures have been taken so far. the second is the crossborder interreg phare programme between greece and bulgaria. the budget for this is eur 254 million and a number of projects have been launched, including transport infrastructure measures. thirdly, the interreg programme for greece and cards for albania and the interreg programme for greece and cards for the former yugoslav republic of macedonia. the programme with albania is to be allocated eur 126 million, and the programme with the former yugoslav republic of macedonia eur 103 million. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. these interreg cards programmes are expected to be converted shortly into neighbourhood programmes to promote and strengthen cooperation between greece and albania and between greece and the former yugoslav republic of macedonia. pre-accession assistance for turkey will start with the 2004 programme and will include a crossborder cooperation component between turkey and greece, currently amounting to around eur 2 million per year. the aim of this project is to improve cross-border cooperation at local level between greece and turkey and to support the further development of the economic potential of the border regions. it is expected that the funding for these activities will be increased in the coming years. the phare programme for crossborder cooperation between bulgaria and greece, currently worth eur 20 million per year, has focused on infrastructure, in particular the rehabilitation of regional roads leading to the two existing border crossings. the commission also plans to co-finance three new border crossings with access roads, one of which is almost complete. over two thirds of the programme's resources have been allocated to this priority. the environment is the second priority, and following the accession of bulgaria and romania, interreg is promoting crossborder cooperation between greece and bulgaria on both sides of the border.
the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment is a very important one. the question i have to ask is clear: will we have a time-bound and differentiated extension of the territorial scope of this really useful community action? the commission itself confirms that the greek regions in question are facing very serious problems in terms of income, labour market dynamics and human resources.

council. - mr president, the issue of interethnic tensions in vojvodina was raised in the relevant council bodies and in the council itself, namely in the general affairs and external relations council on 13 september 2004. the eu heads of mission in belgrade have been asked to prepare a report on the situation in the province, including a general overview of inter-ethnic relations and more information on recent incidents. the heads of mission report was published on 15 september last. it states that although interethnic intolerance appears to have increased in vojvodina, the incidents - however disturbing - have been generally small-scale and do not appear to be showing a clear trend. the report recommends that a follow-up report be drawn up in due course after the local elections, i.e. from 19 september to 3 october. the competent advisory bodies will review the situation in the light of the follow-up report. in its conclusions on the western balkans of 11 october 2004, the general affairs and external relations council welcomed the commitment given by the government of serbia on 8 september to intensify dialogue with all ethnic communities and looked forward to the implementation of that commitment. the eu will continue to monitor developments in this area. respect for human rights and the rights of minorities is one of the fundamental values of the european union and an objective of the union's foreign and security policy. serbia and montenegro do not currently have a contractual relationship with the european union. the commission will prepare a feasibility report in the coming months to assess whether it can recommend to the council to open negotiations with serbia and montenegro on a stabilisation and association agreement.

i need advice. mr president, one of the important elements of the millennium development goal, which is to establish a global partnership for development, is sustainable debt relief. the debt problem of developing countries must be tackled in a broad way by means of national and international measures, with a view to making debt sustainable in the long term. in line with the monterrey consensus, adopted by the heads of state or government of the united nations member states in march 2002, the eu recognises that sustainable debt financing is essential to unlock resources for private and public investment, and that external debt relief can play a crucial role in unlocking resources that can be re-invested in activities that promote sustainable growth and development. the achievement of the millennium development goals is a core objective of the european union and more generally of the international communities. the commitments made by the eu member states at the monterrey conference reflect the union's leading role in the international effort to achieve the millennium development goals and should be reflected in the full range of eu policies in its decisions on the allocation of financial resources. in its conclusions of 14 november 2002, the council confirmed its willingness to, and i quote, 'continue efforts to re-establish debt sustainability in the context of the strengthened hipc initiative, so that developing countries, and in particular the poorest, can continue to pursue growth and development without being hampered by the burden of unsustainable debt'. the eu is also looking at how to go beyond the current hipc initiative. at the high level dialogue on financing for development, held in new york in october 2003 in the framework of the 58th united nations general assembly, the eu encouraged all donor and creditor countries that had not yet done so to take up their share of bilateral debt relief and to contribute to the multilateral financing of the hipc initiative. the eu also stressed the importance for the imf and the world bank to report regularly on the compliance of their member states with their hipc obligations, in particular with regard to article 4 on monitoring. it was also suggested that the formal bilateral debt settlement in the paris club should also take account of debtor countries meeting their hipc obligations as creditors. during that dialogue, the eu also expressed its willingness to discuss with the international financial institutions and other donors possible changes in the calculation of the requirements for additional debt relief - topping up - at the endpoint, for those hipc countries whose debt burden is considered unsustainable at that time due to the severe exogenous shocks. it also showed a willingness to study ways of closing the financial gap while ensuring fair burden-sharing. the eu considers that both hipc countries and donor and creditor countries are responsible for sustainable debt repayment in the long term. the question whether it would be a useful initiative for the european commission to set up a debt department, as the world bank has recently done, is not, as you will understand, within my competence but within the competence of the commission.

the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. first of all, i would like to thank mrs záborská and the members of the committee on women's rights and gender equality for taking up this extremely important issue. i would also like to thank mrs záborská for her very engaging speech. i cannot agree with you, however, when you say in general terms that the european union has failed to create equal opportunities. from the very beginning of the european union, in both the treaty and the legislation, equal opportunities played a very important role and much has been achieved. but it is true that women, even in the european member states, whether they are employed or unemployed, young or old, are more likely than men to be at risk of poverty and thus of social exclusion. many women have no secure income of their own and are financially dependent on their families or on minimum income. many women of retirement age have no or insufficient pension, either because they have not been employed at all or because they have given up, had to give up or have been part-time. what is particularly alarming is that the burden of poverty, which affects the family as a whole, is disproportionately borne by women. the causes of this are, on the one hand, gender specificity in working life and, on the other hand, the gender division of labour in families. single-parent families, as you have mentioned, again in the majority of cases women with children, are very often in financial distress. and we should have a special respect for single mothers. the negative impact is also potentiated for women who belong to vulnerable or marginalised groups such as immigrants, ethnic minorities or people with disabilities. very often these women are victims of multiple discrimination. this is simply no longer acceptable, and our fight against poverty and social exclusion must start primarily from these points. the efforts of the actors at european and national level to combat female poverty, to combat the feminization of poverty, as you have called it, are reflected in the open method of coordination in the field of social inclusion. although this remains primarily a member state responsibility, the european commission and the social protection committee have an important role to play in ensuring that the principle of gender mainstreaming is effectively implemented. the common objectives on which the process is based also include the gender equality aspect. this aspect shall be fully taken into account in the preparation of the national action plan. the aim of the plan is to provide for specific measures for women. in doing so, member states must ensure that ngos dealing with women's specific concerns are principally involved in the development, follow-up and evaluation of the national action plans. the open method of coordination in the fields of poverty and exclusion is complemented by a community action programme until the end of 2006 and by the promotion of cooperation between member states in combating social exclusion. it is worth noting that one criterion for granting grants, for example in the structural funds, is also based on the promotion of gender equality. as mentioned above, the commission is aware that women of retirement age are particularly at risk of poverty. in this context, the open method of coordination has also been applied to issues such as the adequacy of pensions and the financial sustainability of pension systems. a number of initiatives taken at eu level in recent years have helped to effectively implement the concerns expressed by this house in its 1994 resolution on women's poverty in europe. these include, for example, the introduction of the reversal of the burden of proof in gender equality legislation and the recent directive based on article 13 of the ec treaty, in addition to the measures under the open method of coordination. the european employment strategy has incorporated a gender mainstreaming approach and the structural funds reform has further reinforced the gender equality dimension. we can all agree that effective and targeted strategies require reliable and specific data. gender-disaggregated data are crucial for the fight against poverty and social exclusion. they form the basis for sound indicators. the open method of coordination in the area of social exclusion uses a set of common indicators, complemented by national data, as a framework for exchange and analysis. all indicators are to be broken down by gender by default. in addition, the indicators need to be broken down by age to give special consideration to children and child poverty. the commission regularly publishes quantitative information on poverty and social exclusion, broken down by gender and age. i can assure you that the commission is aware of the problems of women's poverty. it also knows that poverty is one of the main foundations and causes of trafficking in women and other forms of sexual exploitation. i would like to stress that none of us, neither the european institutions nor the member states nor society, can accept that women are victims of sexual or other forms of exploitation. we must continue to act decisively and make additional efforts to combat the existing causes. we need to ensure that women have more economic opportunities, gain more economic independence and have full access to economic resources, support services and education.

the commissioner, poverty and social exclusion, where women predominate in the 20 per cent of households, compared with 19 per cent of those headed by men, is the modern form of social misery, the violation of human rights of the 20th and 21st centuries. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. a new social map is needed, based on a specific and integrated strategy to tackle poverty and social exclusion in all social strata, and especially women. at european level, the main policy to combat poverty is the establishment of a minimum income guarantee, which can be implemented by women living below the poverty line. this policy will ensure that all the population enjoys a satisfactory income. the minimum income is the one which should be used as a transitional stage for women's reintegration into employment and combined with development policies within and outside the community, with social security and health, social welfare, housing, child-rearing, education and training, agricultural development and employment policies. the cooperation of all the countries of the european union, of local and regional authorities, is important and necessary to reduce the new features of poverty, migration and the emergence of newly poor groups. the commission is aware that the social policies, the community policies and the structural funds do not give priority to the fight against poverty. but the member states, like the community, are strengthening the competitiveness of the european economy, not social issues such as women and poverty. the modern european model, commissioner, cannot be stigmatised by marginalized social groups, such as women who are at the bottom of the poverty ladder.

in writing. the proposed directive also privatises the certification of seeds for sowing, which is converted into a profit-making business. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the quality of the products is not affected by the quality of the products themselves. the report finds that the new method does not significantly reduce the quality of the seeds! the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. and the question is: why propose to replace a system when the new one not only does not improve the existing one but is worse and more expensive? the aim is to increase the profitability of private capital, which will not only produce and market but also certify and reduce the corresponding public expenditure in the member states, which will be passed on to farmers in large numbers. the penalties provided for in the directive for private individuals are of no significance to farmers, because they neither protect them from damage nor compensate them. in my country, vegetable seeds that were supposedly guaranteed by the production company were degraded, resulting in the farmers who used them going bankrupt, not being compensated and being involved in fruitless and costly legal proceedings. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one.
in writing. the argo programme was adopted in june 2002 in the run-up to the seville conference (known for its anti-migration and repressive decisions), provided for the financing of joint actions by at least three member states in the field of border protection with eur 25 million, and was to run for four years. the funds were not absorbed, not because there was disagreement over the orientation of the programme, but because the ruling class in each eu country wants (for now at least) to be in charge of its own borders and to decide in its own interests how many migrants it is in its interest to let off the hook. the commission's proposal to the council under discussion, however, provides for doubling the initial amount and even funding individual initiatives by each member state, which clearly demonstrates the eu's determination to protect a european area of repression and policing. the pilot project is clearly intended to prepare the ground for what is being done more clearly today in the light of the new, more reactive programme of the hague conference, which succeeded the tampere conference and was decided at the recent summit. the report by parliament is fully in line with the decision of the eu institutions and the commission proposal. in writing. the resolution is a wish list that falls short of the serious problem that threatens the future of the planet. while it is positive that direct benefit measures are supported, they are not explicitly mentioned. they are limited to renewable energy sources, energy efficiency improvements and energy production from biomass, and do not include other measures such as the use of biofuels as a partial substitute for fossil fuels, the use of more of the thermal energy produced in power plants, the improvement of available techniques to emit less greenhouse gases (ghg) per unit of energy produced, the substantial protection and use of forests as a vehicle for co2 in the atmosphere. the eu's efforts to be a 'role model' are undermined by commission figures showing the eu-15 falling far short of the target of reducing ghg emissions by 8% by 2010 compared to 1990. we find our categorical and unwavering opposition to the marketing of the a.t.c. as ineffective and hypocritical. the resolution not only accepts but also encourages the trade in pollutants, proving that multinationals and their political representatives can pollute only if they pay, that is to say, they have little interest in actually reversing this dangerous trend, and we shall vote against it.
