commission mr president, mr rapporteur, the commission has looked with interest at the ombudsman's activity report for 2003. you know that this debate comes at a crucial moment, at a time when the new parliamentary term has just begun and the procedure for appointing the future european ombudsman is under way. during the previous debate on the european ombudsman's activity report for 2002, the commission had the opportunity to congratulate the first european ombudsman, mr söderman, on his role in the establishment of the european system of mediation. and i would like to congratulate his successor, mr diamandouros, who took office in april 2003 and who has made his institution more effective in promoting good governance, respect for the rule of law and human rights. this is a valuable contribution for the commission and for european citizens in general. the commission considers that the development of contacts with national and regional authorities, responsible for mediation between citizens and public administrations in the member states, has improved the role of the european ombudsman and made the institution known to european citizens, as the ombudsman pointed out. this is of paramount importance, because if we have institutions which are not known, they will not be used, and their usefulness will not be demonstrated. the commission, like the ombudsman, takes note of the increase in the number of complaints to the ombudsman and considers that this increase should continue as, in particular, the effects of enlargement become felt. i also agree with the ombudsman that an increase in the number of complaints does not mean that the situation on the ground is not good; it simply means that citizens are becoming more aware of their rights, which is a good thing. as regards the handling of cases, the commission welcomes the fact that in many cases the european authorities have taken appropriate steps to find a solution. as regards the amendment of the ombudsman's statute, it is now for parliament to take the initiative and decide by majority, with the approval of the council, the commission issuing only an opinion. but you can be sure, ladies and gentlemen, that the commission will look at any new initiative carefully and with an open mind. the commission did not want to act hastily on the code of good administrative behaviour, which would be binding on all the institutions and bodies of the european union. the commission's code, which was largely inspired by the ombudsman's proposals, entered into force in november 2000. the first implementation report, which covers 2001 only, does not yet provide the necessary background for the launch of a proposal for an interinstitutional code. we will therefore continue to work on the more comprehensive data base to see if and how the code needs to be reformed. the commission agrees with parliament's proposals concerning epso, the newly created interinstitutional body whose task is to draw up lists of candidates for the general competitions, according to the needs expressed by each institution. the rules and practices on transparency and openness, which have been established over the years, in particular through the ombudsman's work, must be respected in the selection procedures. the commission supports parliament's call on the union's institutions and bodies to implement the regulation on the right of access to documents. the commission recalls that regulation 1049/2001 now also applies to community agencies, which should facilitate the resolution of various complaints. as regards access to documents in the context of infringement proceedings, the commission should have the necessary leeway to conduct its investigations in full autonomy. a balance must be struck between the principle of transparency and the confidentiality required for the dialogue between the commission and the member states. the commission's practice in this area is in accordance with the case law of the court of first instance. for infringement proceedings which are closed, the commission has revised its practice and documents are often made available on a case-by-case basis. that is what the commission wanted to say at the moment, mr rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, in response to the report presented. the commission would also like to express its sincere thanks to the committee on petitions for its excellent work, on behalf of the commission and on behalf of the citizens. i would also like to take this opportunity to remind the president that 15 years ago i was chairman of this committee. the committee on petitions is doing a great job of serving the public.
, on behalf of the team. mr president, the annual report for 2003, which the european ombudsman has submitted to the president of our parliament and presented to the committee on petitions, deserves our approval, ladies and gentlemen, in accordance with the report by mr de rossa and the resolution. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. we must all take note of its key conclusions and take them into account for further action. we must take account of the information provided and ask that the lack of openness and transparency in the functioning of the european institutions be addressed. the fact that 75% of complaints do not fall within the remit of the european ombudsman is not negative for the institution, because they were referred to other bodies. what is important for us, however, is the analysis of 25% of the admissible complaints, two thirds of which concern the european commission, the community institution which takes decisions with an impact on citizens. there were complaints about the european parliament and the council, but also about services such as the personnel selection office. it would seem, however, that even taking into account the 40% increase in complaints announced by mr diamantouros for 2004, few of the 425 million european citizens have recourse to the european ombudsman, an institution which is already celebrating its 10th anniversary and which has been served with acknowledged success and strengthened by the presence of both the benefits of articles 41, 42 and 43 of the charter of fundamental rights, which is now part of the treaty establishing a constitution for europe, as approved by the council. because they may not recognise out-of-court remedies, but if, according to article 1 (2) of the new constitution, decisions should be taken as openly and as closely as possible to the citizen, the role of the european mr president, as a member of the committee on international trade i would like to concentrate my speech on strengthening the role of the european union in a more transparent, more democratically legitimised and efficient world trade system. a system that is at a critical stage of its evolution. there is increasing interdependence in international trade and integration of markets. the number of countries participating in the multilateral cooperation system is also increasing. these two reasons are pressing for an adjustment of the rules and disciplines of the wto, for further opening up of the markets for goods and services, for a greater link between international trade and development. this trend is clearly reflected in the doha mandate. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the european union, the most important actor in the international economy and trade diplomacy, is called upon to play an even more active - i would say leading - role in strengthening and continuing to adapt the wto system. in order for the new globalised environment to optimise the allocation of resources, stability, predictability and solidarity will need to be reinforced by new strong institutions and management mechanisms. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. my proposal concerns the areas of economic and political cooperation of financial markets and international development cooperation on the basis of a model of a new international social and ecological market economy. the modern world needs a new coherent world economic order, a new system of mutually supportive thematic and functional arrangements. i believe that this is the direction in which europe must move and in which the commission - in writing. we consider it appropriate in principle to equalise the participation rate in rural development support programmes of the areas which joined the eu on 1.5.2004 with that of the existing regions. it is surprising, however, that this equalisation will be achieved without any financial cost to the community budget, since the participation of the 10 new countries in the programmes will increase from 80% to 85%, and that this equalisation only concerns the objective 1 regions, while remaining unclear for the other regions. the above-mentioned deliberate 'vagueness' lies in the fact that behind the false formulations of 'support for agriculture' there is an attempt, through these programmes, to bring community agriculture into structural conformity with the requirements of the cap, the wto, multinational and monopoly capital, to redistribute resources between the first and second pillars in order to conceal and mitigate the consequences and to prevent social explosions, since the 'diversification of economic activities in the countryside' sought in practice means a drastic reduction in the decline of agricultural activity and the search for 'resting places' for the young and old farmers who have been uprooted. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rural development programmes are implemented in a way that is compatible with the common agricultural policy.
in writing. the 'europass' is another lever to replace systematic and integrated training, from fragmented skills and learning by deadlines, adapted to current market needs, with the aim of exploiting workers, in full accordance with the lisbon strategy. it gives employers exclusive responsibility for assessing educational and vocational qualifications in order to select the most suitable worker for their company's needs, removing this role from the education system. the 'euro-passport' is open to renewals and additions of more and new requirements by employers, and it tends to become a tool for the personnel's dossier, with descriptions not just of their skills and knowledge, but also of their attitude, beliefs, behaviour. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. the policy of lifelong training and of rotating individual courses hinders rather than facilitates mobility, and signifies the total availability of man to the changing needs of work. we call for the abolition of europass and the introduction of: 1) a uniform, public, free education system to create a comprehensive educational background, substantial vocational training leading to full professional competence, 2) high living standards and equality in terms of work, education and living conditions for migrant workers.
the commission. the recent contamination incident has shown that the european community already has the necessary comprehensive legislation to deal appropriately with similar situations. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. monitoring programmes for dioxins and pcbs in food and feed are in place throughout the european union. these programmes enabled the dutch authorities to identify the contamination and to take appropriate measures to eliminate the source. monitoring and tracing systems are in place to identify potentially infected holdings and livestock units. in order to prevent the introduction of potentially contaminated food and feed into the food chain, the necessary restrictive measures have been taken for those establishments. the early warning system for food and feed has been particularly effective in exchanging information between competent authorities. the introduction of dioxin ceilings by the european union played a major role in the management of this contamination episode. it is indeed a legal tool for decision-making that safeguards the protection of public health. however, it must be admitted that it is not possible to completely rule out the possibility of contamination of the food chain. the danger will always remain and that is why we have taken the necessary measures to identify and deal with such risks. but it is of the utmost importance for the protection of public health that infections are detected at an early stage through surveillance and that we have rules in place to prevent and deter further contamination of the food chain. existing community legislation already provides for such provisions. nevertheless, the contamination incident of today has demonstrated the need to assess the safety not only of the ingredients of food and feed but also of the products directly or indirectly used in the manufacture of food and feed. food law states that food and feed businesses are responsible for product safety. the commission will continue to stress this obligation to undertakings and to monitor the checks carried out under the responsibility of the competent national services to ensure compliance with the provisions in force. finally, as regards landfills, community legislation has been adopted to ensure that landfills are designed and operated in such a way as not to endanger human health or the environment.
madam president, commissioner, the necessary tools for risk management and public health are, on the one hand, the establishment of maximum levels of dioxins in feed materials, feed and food at a strict but feasible level which will become stricter over time and, on the other hand, the search for accountability at all stages of the food chain where dioxins are detected. but the weak link in the chain for the success of this effort is control mechanisms, especially in the feed sector where the link to public health is not directly felt by the citizen and awareness at the production level is lower than in food. this is also shown by the fact that the last major nutritional problems were caused by dioxins in animal feed. what action do you intend to take to strengthen and reinforce the existing model of feed control mechanisms so that controls at both european and member state level are more effective?
commission madam president, ladies and gentlemen, in recent decades humanitarian aid actors have worked in conditions of insecurity and unpredictability. the risks involved in providing aid in unstable situations have - and i think this has not escaped anyone's notice - increased considerably. i take as an example iraq, côte d'ivoire and darfur in particular. humanitarian personnel have themselves been the target of attacks by armed forces and militias all too often. given the difficult conditions in which the commission works through echo, the humanitarian aid office and its partners, the delivery of aid and material assistance to the suffering people is becoming increasingly difficult. furthermore, it should be noted that the increased involvement of non-humanitarian actors in the strict sense, such as the military, in humanitarian activities has sometimes led to confusion of roles, with the resulting risks to the safety of humanitarian personnel. in order to preserve the existence of a humanitarian space and, consequently, to guarantee the delivery of aid and material to the victims, the commission's decisions are inspired by humanitarian values and principles, as well as by the desire to provide quality assistance through its partnership with humanitarian organisations. therefore, in accordance with the council regulations on humanitarian aid, the commission's decisions are dictated solely by humanitarian needs and basic humanitarian principles, namely neutrality, impartiality and independence. respect for these principles should contribute to the protection of humanitarian personnel and access to victims in the event of conflict. in order to maintain the distinction between the role of military personnel and that of humanitarian personnel, the commission supports the fundamental position of civilian organisations in the implementation of humanitarian action in situations where military capability and resources are used. to do so, it maintains that such recourse must be in line with the guidelines for the use of military and civil protection resources in complex emergency humanitarian aid operations conducted by the united nations. echo is not an operational organisation. its mission is to finance the coordinated delivery of humanitarian aid through partner organisations. these include international ngos, other international organisations and the united nations. the quality and effectiveness of echo's assistance depend largely on the professional skills of its partners. all the organisations that sign the partnership agreement under which they work with echo are therefore committed to upholding the fundamental humanitarian values and principles i have just mentioned. the basic objective of humanitarian aid is not to prevent or resolve conflicts but to alleviate the human suffering caused by them. in consultation with other donors and partners, echo is actively seeking ways to address the growing problems of access to victims and the safety of humanitarian personnel, with the aim of improving the situation in this regard. it is important to raise awareness among governments, armed forces and non-state actors of their obligations under international humanitarian law. this right requires parties to a conflict to facilitate the passage of aid convoys and the provision of neutral assistance to civilians affected by a conflict. so, as a living example of this principle, when i went to sudan, to darfur to be more precise, i reminded the government authorities and the rebels that it was absolutely essential to allow free access for international humanitarian organizations to the victims and the people who needed help. this is a fundamental principle and i am giving this example to emphasize the importance of political dialogue between the commission and the authorities of these countries.

mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i feel that we are all living through historic days - citizens and politicians - because the decision which the european council is expected to take on turkey's accession negotiations will be a milestone for turkey itself, the european union and our wider region. no other candidate has been so widely discussed. there have been no such differences between member states, between political parties, no such open pressure from the united states, no such concerns and fears, no such serious and varied questions at every table. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the arguments for and against turkey's membership have been voiced in the past few days. there's one thing we have in common: we call on the european council to defend the dignity of the european union. the european union must not only respect turkey but also the values which unite the peoples of europe. no political realism can be used as an alibi to set aside our values or to accommodate them to turkish demands, whether this is in the spirit and letter of the copenhagen criteria or in the recognition of all 25 members of the european union - i need advice. i am grateful to the president for the comments made by many, which i take note of in preparation for the forthcoming european council on thursday and friday. let me begin with the fundamental debate which some members of this house have been discussing, namely the debate on what the european union is, what europe is. i very much agree with a number of members - mr watson was the first and most explicit in that - that the european union is not a religious community. i also agree with mr cohn-bendit that europe is not a cultural community either. europe, the european union, is, as mr schulz and others have said, a community of values. european integration is not a religious project, but a political one. in this connection, it was a nice remark from mrs uca who, looking at turkey, said that this conclusion can also be a bridge to islam and that we can thus indeed show that europe is a political and not a religious project. conversely, turkey can show that a country which is predominantly islamic can also be a democratic and functioning state governed by the rule of law. some spoke explicitly and appreciatively about the series of conferences on what europe is and what are the values we share. mr maaten and mr lehne have already discussed this. one of the important lessons we can draw from the debate on european values and european identity is that we must be careful to focus on european identity. we all know, after the last century, the negative consequences of a top-down identity. we also recognize that many of the values we share are universal values and we want them to be so. nevertheless, there are european features in the way we deal with these values, in the way we can invoke them and in the way we organise them. there are, however, specific european accents in these values. for example, to mention a very important one, tolerance. that brings me to the enlargement debate which many have spoken about, starting with the debate on turkey. we had that debate yesterday and concluded that we must not only show the concerns but also the great benefits of turkey's eventual accession in the long term. one of the advantages has been mentioned by mr schulz and that is safety. mr watson noted that turkey met the criteria sufficiently and that, in line with the commission's conclusion, this was also the position of the presidency after the entry into force of the six laws. mr eurlings and others have again stressed the importance of continuing to exert pressure on turkey in order to achieve the necessary developments in that country. the european union has a duty to protect the rights of the peoples of the world. the presidency has therefore chosen to present a new framework for the forthcoming accession negotiations on the basis of the commission's proposals. that framework allows for more pressure and provides more guarantees for a smooth running of the process. it is precisely on the sensitive political criteria that the so-called 'emergency' option, the suspension option, offers guarantees. for understandable reasons, the cyprus question has been raised again by a number of members of this parliament. mr barroso mentioned it in his opening remarks, as did mr adamou and mr dimitrakopoulos. i would like to reiterate once again the commitment of the dutch presidency to this point in the preparations for the council meeting at the end of this week. we do indeed want turkey to recognise the fact that cyprus is a member of the european union and one of the 25 countries. we want to achieve this by means of turkey's signing of the protocol to the association agreement. mr van den berg has explicitly asked whether the presidency can confirm that the commitment is that no final date for accession is mentioned. very specifically: that is indeed the commitment of the presidency. another question, focusing on the political criteria, was whether the presidency could confirm that turkey would not join if it was not ready. i would like to emphasize that. turkey will not intervene. finally, with regard to turkey, the important and sensitive point which will shortly be discussed again in parliament, namely the so-called open-ended nature of the negotiations with turkey. here again, the question from mr van den berg is explicit: what is the commitment of the presidency? let me make this clear once again: the presidency's commitment is that it is accession negotiations, and accession negotiations are about accession. that's point one. secondly, following the commission's comments, it is an open-ended process. there is no guarantee as to the outcome. the third element is that european solidarity must remain sufficiently guaranteed in all cases, but the commitment of the dutch presidency to the conclusions of thursday and friday is: we do not have a plan b. we shall speak of romania and bulgaria later. i shall confine myself to a few comments on the questions. mr watson and mr becsey, among others, have called for a separate approach for bulgaria on the one hand and romania on the other. in this connection it is worth pointing out that, as you know, the treaty does not provide for any distinction to be made, but that it does provide for a distinction to be made, if necessary, as regards the time of accession, since the possibility has been introduced and agreed to proceed, if there are reasons for it, to a maximum of a one-year delay for the country concerned. that is also the answer to the question put by mr eurlings, mrs oomen-ruijten and mr kirkhope, who are still very concerned about the situation in romania and stress the importance of maintaining maximum pressure on developments in romania. i agree with that and that is why we, as the presidency, have proposed that the council should also conclude that we want additional provisions on monitoring and concrete benchmarks for romania, coupled with the possibility of a sanction of, if necessary, a maximum of one year's delay. croatia has been mentioned by a number of members and is not on the agenda separately, so i think that despite the noise in the chamber i would do well to give you a careful answer to that. mr pinheiro and mr becsey have indicated that croatia has made great progress in its development towards europe and that it is a european country. i want to emphasize both. great progress has been made and the country is at the centre of europe. in addition, mr watson, mr van den berg and mr swoboda rightly pointed out that full cooperation with the icty is of the utmost importance. of course, it would be good, and i say this to mr swoboda and mr becsey, if we could take that positive step, also because of the role model that croatia could thus fulfil in the balkans. i agree with mr swoboda that we must try to make that decision positive. that is the commitment of the presidency and it means that we can indeed take a positive decision on the start of negotiations early next year. but explicitly linked to full cooperation with the icty. another important point that many of you have raised is the issue of combating terrorism. the great importance of this has also been underlined by mr barroso and mr pinheiro and others. in addition, mr watson, mrs malmström and mrs buitenweg have all stressed - and i think rightly - the importance of a balance between the fight against terrorism and fundamental rights. i agree with that and i think mr maaten was right to point out that we have shown in the hague programme that the council also wants to maintain that balance. mr eurlings asked specifically about the possibilities of banning organisations. he will also know that this is a responsibility, a competence, of the member states, but we do ensure that information is exchanged between member states on this point. the exchange of information is crucial in the fight against terrorism, as mr eurlings and others have mentioned. that is a key point of this presidency and is also on the agenda of the council for thursday and friday. we have made concrete progress in this half-year in facilitating the exchange of criminal records, in pooling intelligence and in the situation centres and the threat analyses that can be carried out there. as you know, we will ask the commission to come forward with a more detailed proposal for the exchange of information, as also provided for in the hague programme. as requested by some of the members, i would like to make it clear that the european council intends to take a decision in principle on the appointment of a european human rights ambassador on thursday and friday. the financial perspective has been discussed by many in this parliament. in his introductory remarks, mr barroso rightly spoke of the importance of added value in the context of the multiannual budget and budgetary discipline. he also expressed concern about the 1% approach and the importance of sufficient funds for europe's priorities. many after him have underlined that. it is therefore appropriate to state the council's position on this point. we support many of the priorities mentioned here, but it is still important that in any financial discussion we identify the posteriorities as well as the priorities and not just list the priorities. that is what we have done as the presidency in our so-called building blocks approach, in which we have tried to make an inventory and to sum up not only the priorities but also the backlogs. i can reassure some members who were worried about what is going to happen on thursday and friday. mrs grossetête feared that we would take a decision on 1%, which would not be understood by the citizens. the council has no position on the 1%, however, let that be very clear. the council has identified the positions of the member states, both the priorities and the backlogs, on the basis of the commission's proposals - which is and will remain the starting point, as the new parliament rightly insists - and we will decide on this. i would like to share mr watson's concern about financial accountability and his concern that the court of auditors has not given a in writing. the european parliament's recommendation has ignored the recent annual report of the echr, which is the catalyst for the so-called dutch experiment, and rejects the argument that cannabis is supposedly harmless. in the recommendation, in the name of a nonexistent realism, the nature of the problem is distorted from social to medical, leading to submission and defeat. the author attempts to back door the generalization of the dutch experiment by adopting the dangerous views of dividing drugs into soft and hard. it uses the word 'consumers' instead of 'victim-users' and proposes the establishment of official dosing sites. the result of this dangerous approach is that all the proposals that undermine the primary concern - prevention - focus on reducing the consequences of the damage (replacement programmes), and seek solutions in the mechanisms and instruments of the eu. the link between drug trafficking and the financing of so-called terrorism is an argument for imperialist propaganda and an alibi against the peoples. we have no tolerance for drug culture. the causes of addiction are not divided into "hard" and "soft". we need prevention, treatment and rehabilitation of drug users, and a movement that will fight to overturn the social causes, and the barbaric policy of capitalism that leads to drugs.

council. the council would like to inform the honourable member that the implementation of the idabc programme falls within the remit of the commission, which is assisted in this by the pan-european egovernment services committee. the council would also like to remind the honourable member that the idabc programme decision will only enter into force on 1 january 2005 and thus the implementation of the programme as such has not yet started. the council invites the honourable member to refer this matter to the commission for more details regarding the preparation of the programme. the council is aware of two studies launched by the commission under the ida programme. one study aims to establish the needs and benefits to businesses and citizens of pan-european egovernment services in order to reinforce a properly oriented approach to the delivery of a pan-european service. the second one aims to identify future infrastructure services needed to support the pan-european egovernment services. likewise, in the preparation of the idabc programme, the council also acknowledges the organisation of by commission of a conference due to take place in brussels on 17 and 18 february 2005, which will focus on the requirements for pan-european egovernment services for citizens and businesses and on the factors needed to implement them.
council. apart from these major actions, the ida unit is preparing to launch the programme fully during the first half of 2005. to that end, anticipated activities are being analysed and formats for work programme entries, project implementation plans, etc. are being developed.
council. as the council has repeatedly said, safety and security lie at the heart of the european union maritime policy. measures to improve them, as well as to protect the marine environment, represent the bulk of legislation adopted in this area in the five years since the erika disaster. in its response to this and other similar incidents, the council has consistently supported a dual approach: on the one hand, incorporating the provisions of international conventions into community law and thus ensuring stricter compliance with the highest global standards and, on the other hand, adopting specific community provisions wherever imo standards are lacking or insufficient. in this way a comprehensive body of maritime safety legislation has been built up and is continuing to expand. amongst the specific measures adopted in the intermediate aftermath of the erika disaster, were firstly those under the erika i package aimed at enhanced port state control to combat substandard shipping; stricter monitoring of the classification companies use to conduct safety checks on ships; and the accelerated phasing-out of single-hull oil tankers. secondly, there were those under the erika ii package aimed at the establishment of a community vessel traffic monitoring and information system and the creation of a european maritime safety agency whose main role is to monitor the effectiveness of the eu maritime safety rules in general. other measures include those designed to improve the safety of ships other than oil tankers, such as passenger vessels, to ensure a minimum level of seafarers' training and to reduce pollution from ships. for improving security in the wake of a number of terrorist outrages, a regulation on enhancing ship and port facilities security was adopted earlier this year, incorporating the solas isps measures into eu legislation and expanding them to include domestic traffic. currently under consideration by the council are measures to implement the imo international safety management code within the community, so penalties can be imposed for certain pollution offences and port security enhanced. finally, looking ahead, a new package of maritime safety measures is expected to be submitted for examination by the council in spring 2005.

council. from its outset, at the beginning of the year, the darfur crisis has been one of the absolute priorities on the eu agenda. throughout the year the european union has been at the forefront of international efforts to contain and resolve the conflict in darfur, to stop the massive human rights violations taking place there and to prevent an already very serious humanitarian situation from deteriorating further. member states and the community have contributed to the humanitarian relief efforts with more than eur 325 million. the union has also strongly supported the african union's leading role in addressing the crisis. the eu has thus played a key role in support of an au-led ceasefire monitoring mission and is now actively supporting the deployment of the expanded mission. almost 60 per cent of the total cost of the expanded au mission is financed by a contribution of some eur 80 million from the peace facility, and several member states are providing additional financial and logistical support for the mission as well as planners and military observers. the council has repeatedly, most recently on the occasion of its meeting on 22 november, confirmed its readiness to continue to provide support for the deployment of the mission in terms of financing, logistics and material, as well as personnel if requested. the council considers that the deployment of the expanded au mission in darfur, together with the eu-led negotiations between the parties to find a political solution for darfur, offers an opportunity for a comprehensive solution to the darfur conflict. in parallel to these efforts, the eu has contributed to the pressure from the international community on all parties to guarantee free and unconditional access for humanitarian aid and to ensure the security of the civilian population. the council is closely monitoring compliance by all parties with the demands expressed by the eu and the rest of the international community and the commitments it has undertaken with the un. to this end, the eu collaborates closely with the un secretary-general's special representative for the sudan and actively takes part in the work of the joint implementation mechanism that evaluates the implementation of the joint communiqué which consists of the commitments of both the sudanese government and the united nations. in line with resolutions 1556 and 1564 of the un security council, the council has also on several occasions reiterated its willingness to take appropriate measures, including sanctions, if no progress is achieved on the commitments made by the sudanese government.
council. i agree. as you may know, last monday the council also discussed the situation in sudan and we are seriously concerned about the deterioration of the security and humanitarian situation in darfur. the council therefore called on all parties to abide in full by their commitments. we also encouraged them to conclude the negotiations between the government of sudan and the splma before the end of the year. the council will continue to monitor the situation in darfur and will consider taking appropriate measures, which could include sanctions in accordance with un security council resolutions 1556 and 1565 if no tangible progress is achieved in this respect. hence the situation is and remains high on the agenda of the european union.

states have introduced the aforementioned bac levels so far. however, the council would like to draw the honourable member's attention to the commission recommendation of 6 april 2004 on enforcement context it refers to the council conclusions on road safety from june and december last year, both of which explicitly refer to stricter enforcement, in particular seatbelt use, speeding and drink driving. in the light of this ongoing commitment the council had an exchange of views during its meeting on 9 and 10 december 2004 and adopted the council conclusions on road safety, in which it agreed on the need to focus road safety policies in four important areas, namely enforcement and best practices, vehicle safety, improving safety on european roads and funding road safety. in this context the council underlined that, with regard to road safety enforcement, priority should be given, notably, to driver behaviour that causes high accident risks or could have severe consequences such as driving under the influence of alcohol.

council. the council notes that increasing overall expenditure on research is indeed a central element in the lisbon strategy and one of the keys to making the eu more competitive in the global economy. the council and member states have repeatedly committed themselves to achieving average research and development expenditure of 3 % of gdp by 2010. the recent kok report once again highlighted the need to make good the under-performance in relation to this target. notwithstanding this general statement, the council does not share the honourable member's view that it is the stability and growth pact which is preventing member states from achieving the lisbon objective. perhaps more fundamentally, the stability and growth pact should be used as a tool to encourage national expenditure on individual budget components over and above others. firstly, it is clearly possible to fund research while respecting the pact. this is a very important point that leads on to the second point that the pact is not the factor limiting investment in research. research funding is rather a matter of national priorities. thirdly, i would note that the level of r&d expenditure is not solely dependent on public finance. the target covers privately-funded r&d as well, which can be encouraged by a number of means in addition to public expenditure. the key to reaching the 3 % target is the two thirds of r&d spending that should be privately funded, not the one third that should come from public funds. finally, i would like to recall that the pact is a framework for the management of budgetary policies at global level, with a view to ensuring the sustainability of public finances over the longer term. all expenditure has to be paid for, even if in the longer run we would hope that investment would have positive returns. when the ecofin council discussed the pact on 16 november 2004 and this precise issue was raised, there was a broad majority of member states against pursing the possibility suggested by the member and excluding certain categories of expenditure from deficit calculations. in the analytical assessment of the reasons for an excessive deficit, the council will always take into account the type of expenditure and the likely long-term impact. but the council does not share the honourable member's assessment regarding the exclusion of r&d expenditure from deficit calculations.
mr president, i could have agreed with mr nicolaï on everything he has said if i had not thought that it is not possible to achieve the lisbon objectives without research going too far. i would therefore agree with you if the answer were to be any other subject. but i'm sorry, but i have to disagree because research is the foundation of lisbon.
commission. mr president, i am pleased that parliament supports the commission's proposal for the extension of the community action programme on civil protection for the years 2005 and 2006. i very much appreciate the efforts that have gone into this report, in particular the work of mr papadimoulis, the rapporteur. before i go into detail on this report, i think it is worth recalling the work done under the auspices of this action plan since its adoption in december 1999. the objectives of the civil protection action programme are to help to ensure better protection for people, the environment and property in the event of natural or technological disasters across the community. the action programme has funded a range of useful projects, exchanges of experts and workshops that build invaluable cooperation and also mutual assistance between the member states' civil protection services. the action programme supports and supplements efforts at national, regional and local levels. emphasis has been put on major projects concerning prevention, information of the public, crisis management, disaster medicine and networking among the different volunteer associations active on the ground. in recent years we have seen major disasters. you will recall the floods in central and eastern europe in the summer of 2002, the prestige oil spill, also in the same year, the unprecedented heatwave and devastating forest fires in southern europe in the summer of 2003. this year we were shocked by the violent terrorist attack in madrid and saddened by the earthquakes in iran and morocco. these events clearly illustrated the need for further action to strengthen prevention and preparedness measures and also to enhance our ability to respond rapidly and in solidarity as a european union. the commission believes that major progress has been made in recent years in the contribution and role of the eu in civil protection. the rapporteur has rightly highlighted some issues in the report, and these are both valuable and constructive remarks that we will take into account in our future work. i would like to react immediately to a number of points. the report recommends 'better sharing of information on available resources, expertise and successful strategies and practices at national level'. the commission agrees that our database of resources should be further upgraded and contain more data on responsive capacities, associated costs and available equipment. the commission is currently working with member states on these issues. the report argues for an improved operational framework for volunteers and for the establishment of guidelines for their work. this is a helpful suggestion and echoes the emphasis that the commission is now putting on the work on this subject, which was and will remain a priority for financing in 2005. let me turn to the issue of financing assistance during emergencies. this is an issue of concern to us, but depends on having a sufficient level of funding available. for the time being the budgetary resources available only permit financing of experts who travel to the scene of disasters to assess needs for assistance. additional financing depends at present on the donating and receiving member states. i should like to add that there is also, as you know, the possibility to use the eu solidarity fund to provide compensation to a country suffering severe damage from a disaster. finally, the report asks the commission to provide a report on the programme in 2006. we have no difficulty in accepting this request. with these remarks, i reiterate my thanks to the rapporteur and to parliament as a whole for this thoughtful and constructive opinion. may i add that my colleague, commissioner dimas, sends his apologies for not being here. he is in buenos aires and asked me to replace him.

efficient operation of the mechanism and the extension of the programme are also appropriate, and we support them. in recent years we have all experienced, within the european union and around the world, disasters with far-reaching and direct consequences for the lives of a large number of our fellow citizens and for society and the economy in general. in response to these events, we adopted the community action programme. as we all remember, events have caught up with us. we reacted afterwards. let us today take a good qualitative and quantitative look at the situation and let us see how we can improve the existing mechanism in all its functions and at all stages, from prevention to treatment and rehabilitation and reintegration into society. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial instruments are used effectively. the rapporteur proposes better exchange of information and good practice, more training for intervention teams, mobilisation of volunteers, better coordination between the actors involved at european, national, regional and local level. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the common organization of the market in bananas is a very good one. the matter we are discussing tonight i hope will find the council's agreement because it is a very important matter. it not only solves everyday problems but also has great symbolic meaning. the citizens feel that the european union is intervening in their lives in this way. the european citizenship thus acquires a specific and deeper meaning. such a programme brings together directly european institutions, national and local bodies and local organisations, something we are lacking both at national level and in coordination between the european union and national bodies. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the european union's external action programme for the period 2000 to 2006 is a good one, but it is not enough to make the european union more visible to the outside world, and it is also a good way of showing its human face, which is what you are trying to achieve, commissioner, as i heard you say in the previous report.
in writing. we would like to point out that funding for natural and technological disaster response is so limited that it effectively cancels out any possibility of meaningful intervention and effectiveness. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and that the community's financial resources are used to the full. but how can this happen when everything is judged by strict economic criteria and by the interests of capital? the commission has already taken the initiative in the form of a proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster. when funds are not allocated for flood control projects, when seismic standards are not respected, when forests are left to the mercy of arsonists and land-grabbers, when ship owners are left homeless and unpunished after enormous ecological disasters, what protection can we talk about? how can effective civil protection be organised when public goods and services needed in critical situations are sold to private individuals or converted into repressive mechanisms (see firefighting)? and on the other hand, even these scraps of so-called "recovery" how are they exploited? if you want a shocking answer , ask the greek earthquake victims of 1999 who still live in containers !
the people of zimbabwe have the right and the responsibility to solve their political problems, and no one, much less the european union, the united kingdom and the united states of america, has the right to intervene politically, economically and militarily in zimbabwe or any other country. it is precisely because the people of zimbabwe have achieved independence from britain after a long and bloody struggle and because they are claiming their land, the richest part of which still belongs to foreigners and mainly to the british as a result of colonialism, that a new plan of intervention is being prepared which aims to turn it into a modern colony. the european parliament has been asked to take a decision on the matter, and i am sure that the commission will be able to give its opinion on it. a network of so-called 'non-governmental organisations' is being set up, most of which are shadow organisations and have nothing to do with the popular and social movement but are preparing the ground for interventions. the resolution is unacceptable to the communist party of greece, which has even withdrawn the complaint of arms trafficking by british agents. to end sanctions, to support the people of zimbabwe in their struggle to maintain their independence against neo-colonialism.
on behalf of the team. mr president, twenty years have passed since the bhopal tragedy in india, with its enormous humanitarian and ecological consequences, and i think it is particularly important that the political groups have taken the initiative in adopting a resolution in the european parliament. but, mr president, it is not enough to simply call to mind, it is not enough to denounce the often unfair way in which multinational companies operate in developing countries, it is not enough to express our humanitarian sensitivity in words. the tragedy of this kind may be experienced again by people in other parts of the world if there is no international mobilization to set up international control mechanisms for the operation of multinational companies, to protect the environment, to protect human rights and workers' rights. the european union's role in the un and other international organisations can be more effective here too. the draft resolution, mr president, sets out the problems which continue to exist and which many colleagues have spoken of in the wider bhopal area. in my view, however, it does not fully reflect the efforts made by the federal and regional authorities in india over the past few years in the areas of medical, economic, social and environmental rehabilitation. and important policies have been put in place that we should not ignore. in conclusion, mr president, i believe that the best way to express our sympathy to the victims and their families is for the european commission to take initiatives in cooperation with the world health organization to implement medical and environmental rehabilitation programmes.
